```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0001
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 17c-17d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 17c-17d
  start_marker: 17c
  end_marker: 17d
  start_char: 682
  end_char: 1307
  text_sha256: 9c325f64490fae81456c9b4da6ee51e68e2fa3f44afc0eb1ef2cbdd2449bdcc7
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates opens by referring to a previous day's discussion, summarizing its main topic ('head' / kephalaion) and asking Timaeus to confirm that it was spoken to everyone's satisfaction.
textual_basis: At 17c, Socrates says 'yesterday somewhere of the speeches spoken by me concerning the constitution the head was what sort and from what sort of men the best would appear to me to come to be,' and Timaeus confirms that it was spoken to the satisfaction of all.
limits: This observation records the frame reference; it does not adjudicate whether the referenced discussion is the Republic or a distinct earlier conversation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0002
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 17c-17d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 17c-17d
  start_marker: 17c
  end_marker: 17d
  start_char: 682
  end_char: 1307
  text_sha256: 9c325f64490fae81456c9b4da6ee51e68e2fa3f44afc0eb1ef2cbdd2449bdcc7
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates recapitulates parts of the previous day's city-foundation argument: the separation of farmers and other crafts from the warrior class, the assignment of one craft per person according to nature, and the description of guardians as sole defenders of the city."
textual_basis: At 17c-17d, Socrates asks whether they first separated the class of farmers and other crafts from the race of those who would fight on behalf of the city, and then asserts that by giving each one pursuit according to nature, one craft each, they said the guardians must be only defenders of the city, judging gently those within.
limits: This observation records recapitulation as a dramatic device; it does not claim the prior discussion is definitively the Republic, nor does it assess the truth of the recapitulated claims.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Timaeus confirms that the previous day's discussion was spoken to everyone's satisfaction, establishing a shared starting point for the present conversation.
textual_basis: "At 17c, Timaeus responds to Socrates' summary: 'Yes indeed to us, Socrates, having been spoken to the satisfaction of all' (kai mala ge hēmin, ō Sōkrates, rhētheisa pasin kata noun)."
limits: This observation notes the procedural function of Timaeus's confirmation; it does not claim any unstated reservations or interpretive commitments beyond the explicit agreement.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0004
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 18a-18b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 18a-18b
  start_marker: 18a
  end_marker: 18b
  start_char: 1307
  end_char: 2156
  text_sha256: f3505be3f6b655727fd328b3ab5c0953d56bcda0fdedf93e38d4285632176488
greek_terms:
  - θυμοειδῆ
  - φιλόσοφον
english_gloss: spirited and philosophic
observation: "Socrates recapitulates the guardian nature requirement from the Republic: the guardians' soul must be both spirited (thumoeides) and philosophic (philosophon) in a distinguished way."
textual_basis: At 18a Socrates says they described a certain nature of the guardians' soul that must be at once thumoeides and at once philosophon in a distinguished way. Timaeus confirms with nai.
limits: This is a recapitulation of a prior logos, not a new argument. The observation records that the dual soul-nature profile is repeated; it does not assess whether the Timaeus version differs from Republic details.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0005
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 18a-18b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 18a-18b
  start_marker: 18a
  end_marker: 18b
  start_char: 1307
  end_char: 2156
  text_sha256: f3505be3f6b655727fd328b3ab5c0953d56bcda0fdedf93e38d4285632176488
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates recapitulates the guardian education program: upbringing in gymnastic and music and whatever studies belong to these, and the prohibition of private property with communal living."
textual_basis: At 18a-b Socrates asks whether the guardians were not to be raised in gymnastic and music and whatever studies pertain to them, and then recounts that those so raised should hold no gold, silver, or other private possession but receive a measured wage as auxiliaries, spend in common, live together, and have care for virtue continuously while keeping leisure from other pursuits. Timaeus confirms at 18b that these things were said in that way.
limits: This is a summary recollection, not a new legislative act within the Timaeus. The observation notes the recapitulation; it does not analyze differences from the Republic account.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0006
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 18a-18b

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

  text_sha256: f3505be3f6b655727fd328b3ab5c0953d56bcda0fdedf93e38d4285632176488
greek_terms:
  - ἐλέγομεν
  - ἐλέχθη
english_gloss: we were saying, it was said
observation: Socrates recapitulates the prior day's discourse on the city and guardians, and Timaeus confirms each point with short assent markers.
textual_basis: "Throughout 18a-18b Socrates uses past-tense verbs of speaking (elegomen, elechthe) to recall the previous day's account of the city. Timaeus responds with minimal assents: pantapasi men oun, nai, panu men oun. This structure marks a formal recapitulation before the dialogue's main cosmological discourse begins."
limits: This observation identifies the recapitulation structure. It does not analyze whether any details are omitted or altered from the Republic account.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0007
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 17a-17b

greek_terms:
  - εἷς, δύο, τρεῖς
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates opens by counting the participants (one, two, three) and noting that a fourth person from the previous day's gathering is absent, replaced by those who will now host the discussion.
textual_basis: The first line at 17a has Socrates enumerate the present company and ask after the missing fourth, referencing yesterday's feast and today's hosts. Timaeus at 17b confirms the reciprocal hospitality framework and asks Socrates to recap the previous day's discussion.
limits: This observation records the opening dramatic setup and cast enumeration; it does not analyze the philosophical content or the identity of the absent fourth.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0008
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 17a-17b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

  start_marker: 17a
  end_marker: 17b

  text_sha256: 96ff2a9407bd9906e0158ee10052d5fac3c07af445173f85bbb1b8a0ad6df0a1
greek_terms:
  - ἀσθένειά τις αὐτῷ συνέπεσεν
english_gloss: ""
observation: A fourth participant from the previous day's gathering is reported absent at 17a due to illness, and is never named in this opening exchange.
textual_basis: Timaeus reports at 17a that an illness befell the missing person, who is referred to only as the fourth (tetartos) and as the absent one (tou apontos) at 17a. Socrates then assigns Timaeus and the others to fill the vacancy.
limits: This records the unnamed absent character as a dramatic device; it does not speculate on the identity or symbolic meaning of the absence.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0009
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 17a-17b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 17a-17b

greek_terms:
  - δίκαιον
  - ξενίοις
  - ἀνταφεστιᾶν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Timaeus frames the present discussion as a reciprocal hospitality exchange: after Socrates hosted the previous day with fitting guest-gifts of speech, the remaining speakers should now return the feast of discourse."
textual_basis: At 17b, after Socrates asks whether the remaining speakers must fill in for the absent fourth participant, Timaeus says it would not be just for those hosted yesterday with fitting guest-gifts not to return the feast eagerly.
limits: This records the reciprocal hospitality frame for the dialogue opening. It does not treat the exchange as a legal case, and it does not summarize the content of the prior day's discussion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0010
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 18c-18d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 18c-18d
  start_marker: 18c
  end_marker: 18d
  start_char: 2156
  end_char: 3055
  text_sha256: afac6cf934c7bf4e838afec888f392e5e290cdbec90ed05fc21b6e97892d3cee
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates recapitulates two provisions of the city-in-speech from the previous day's conversation: (1) the common nature and pursuits of women and men, and (2) the community of marriages and children where no parent knows their own offspring."
textual_basis: At 18c Socrates explicitly recalls these provisions using phrases that mark them as already agreed, for example 'we mentioned concerning women.' Socrates uses the term 'easy to remember' (18c and 18d) and asks 'do we remember' (18d). Timaeus confirms at 18c that these things were spoken in that way and at 18d affirms they are easy to remember.
limits: The recapitulation covers only the two provisions about women and procreation. It does not summarize every detail of the previous day's city-in-speech discussion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0011

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 18d Socrates transitions from recapitulating the previous day's city-in-speech provisions to a new topic: the need for rulers to arrange marriages secretly so that the best natures are produced as much as possible."
textual_basis: Socrates marks the transition at 18d by shifting from 'do we not remember' to a procedural question about how the offspring might straightaway become as good as possible in their natures, then begins to recount what the rulers were said to need to do regarding marriage arrangements.
limits: The segment ends at 18d before Socrates completes his summary of the marriage-arrangement proposal, so this observation captures the transition marker rather than the full content of the new topic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0012

  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Timaeus gives only minimal assent responses to Socrates' recapitulation, confirming the previous day's account without adding or questioning anything.
textual_basis: Timaeus responds at 18c with a brief confirmatory clause that these things were spoken in that way and at 18d with a short affirmative plus endorsement of memorability. Both responses simply confirm Socrates' recollection without elaboration or challenge.
limits: This observation covers only Timaeus' role in this segment. The pattern of minimal assent does not necessarily characterize his speech behavior elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0013
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 18e-19a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 18e-19a
  start_marker: 18e
  end_marker: 19a
  start_char: 3055
  end_char: 3664
  text_sha256: 991c21c28706bf3afc0afbc77575d568f36206473872b3a92634ed7871d3d4d6
greek_terms:
  - κλήροις
  - τύχην ἡγουμένοις αἰτίαν
english_gloss: by lots...considering chance the cause of the pairing
observation: At 18e, Socrates describes using a lottery device to pair good and bad citizens so that each pair with similar natures, and adds that they should attribute the outcome to chance to avoid enmity. The explicit rationale is that the rulers manipulate the lottery so that citizens believe chance, not design, governs the pairing.
textual_basis: Socrates says the rulers must contrive by lots so that the bad separately and the good will each pair with similar natures, and adds lest any enmity arise among them through these things, believing chance to be the cause of the pairing (18e-19a). The verb for contrive indicates deliberate design; the citizens are to regard the lottery outcome as chance.
limits: This observation notes the explicit design of a masking device in the reported ideal city. It does not claim that this reflects Socrates' or Plato's own view of the dialogue's structure, nor that the dialogue itself employs an esoteric masking strategy.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0014
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 18e-19a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 18e-19a
  start_marker: 18e
  end_marker: 19a
  start_char: 3055
  end_char: 3664
  text_sha256: 991c21c28706bf3afc0afbc77575d568f36206473872b3a92634ed7871d3d4d6
greek_terms:
  - ὡς ἐν κεφαλαίοις πάλιν ἐπανελθεῖν
english_gloss: to go back over it in summary form
observation: At 19a, Socrates proposes to recapitulate the previous day's discussion in summary form. The phrase explicitly marks the preceding material (18e-19a) as a compressed summary of a longer earlier conversation, embedding it within a reported-dialogue frame.
textual_basis: Socrates asks whether they have now gone through it, just as yesterday, to go back over it again in summary form (19a). The phrase 'just as yesterday' and the recapitulation language mark the material as a second-order report of a prior conversation.
limits: This observation identifies the framing device; it does not assess the accuracy or completeness of the summary relative to the Republic, nor does it claim that the summary is deliberately distorted.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0015
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 19d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 19d
  start_marker: 19d
  end_marker: 19d
  start_char: 4517
  end_char: 4902
  text_sha256: 78295b3b63df3f14988d3ad0150a787d81f7240d5f673ff9604a8cbfb70a09a7
greek_terms:
  - ἐμαυτοῦ
  - μή ποτ’ ἂν δυνατὸς γενέσθαι
  - ἱκανῶς ἐγκωμιάσαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates disavows his own ability to praise the men and the city adequately, then generalizes the difficulty to poets whose imitation is shaped by their upbringing.
textual_basis: At 19d Socrates says he has judged himself unable to become capable of adequately praising the men and city. He then says this is not surprising in his own case and extends the judgment to poets, because the mimetic tribe most easily and best imitates what it has been reared among.
limits: This records an encomiastic capacity disavowal, not a claim that Socrates lacks knowledge in general. The continuation develops the contrast with sophists and the interlocutors' qualifications.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0016
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 19d-19e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 19d-19e
  start_marker: 19d
  end_marker: 19e
  start_char: 4517
  end_char: 5348
  text_sha256: 9546ad03ebc565156602b8f152c28f2aad91d1d7ad1f826d2c3cec9219ddf897
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates argues that the mimetic tribe imitates best what it has been reared in, and that what lies outside its upbringing is difficult to imitate well in deeds and still harder in words.
textual_basis: At 19d-e, Socrates states that the mimetic tribe imitates most easily and best what it has been reared in, while what lies outside its rearing is hard to imitate well in deeds and even harder in words.
limits: This observation concerns a general claim about mimesis tied to upbringing, not a direct critique of poetry in the Republic; the claim is deployed to explain why poets cannot adequately praise the ideal city.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0017
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 19d-19e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 19d-19e
  start_marker: 19d
  end_marker: 19e
  start_char: 4517
  end_char: 5348
  text_sha256: 9546ad03ebc565156602b8f152c28f2aad91d1d7ad1f826d2c3cec9219ddf897
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates characterizes the sophist tribe as experienced in many fine speeches but, because they are wanderers without settled homes, likely to miss the mark concerning what philosophical and political men do and say in war and battle.
textual_basis: At 19e, Socrates says the sophist tribe is experienced in many fine speeches but fears they may miss the mark concerning philosophical and political men, because they wander through cities and have no private dwellings.
limits: This observation records Socrates's characterization of sophists as a class in contrast to philosophers and political men; it does not evaluate the accuracy of the characterization.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0018
temp_id: temp_20ab_01
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 20a-20b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 20a-20b
  start_marker: 20a
  end_marker: 20b
  start_char: 5348
  end_char: 6187
  text_sha256: 5fdd779c2dc084afbe5939e3719d02e6827956ebb344dd9e1180b965a3c3435e
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates describes each of his three interlocutors — Timaeus, Critias, and Hermocrates — in turn, cataloguing their qualifications of nature and nurture for the discourse he is about to invite.
textual_basis: At 20a Socrates says Timaeus comes from the well-governed Locris, holds highest offices and honors, and has reached the summit of philosophy; of Critias he says all Athenians know he is no layman in the matters under discussion; of Hermocrates he says nature and nurture are adequate according to many witnesses (20a-20b).
limits: The observation records the formal prosopographic catalogue; it does not assess whether Socrates' descriptions are accurate or ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0019

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates recalls the prior day's agreement: he narrated a discourse on the constitution, and the interlocutors promised to return a sequel discourse."
textual_basis: At 20b Socrates says 'yesterday I, when you asked, gladly narrated the matters concerning the constitution' (chthes ego dianooumenos, hymon deomenon ta peri tes politeias dielthein), and that in return they agreed to deliver the sequel (ton hexes logon).
limits: The observation identifies the reported-dialogue frame structure; it does not determine whether the constitution discourse references the Republic or another work.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0020
temp_id: temp_20ab_03
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 20a-20b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 20a-20b
  start_marker: 20a
  end_marker: 20b

  end_char: 6187
  text_sha256: 5fdd779c2dc084afbe5939e3719d02e6827956ebb344dd9e1180b965a3c3435e
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates frames the current discourse as a reciprocal obligation: having fulfilled the requested narration himself, he now demands the agreed-upon sequel from the interlocutors."
textual_basis: At 20b Socrates states that he gladly fulfilled their request yesterday (prothymos echarizomen), and having done what was asked, he in turn demanded from them what he now asks again (eipon de tapitachthenta antepetaxa hymin ha kai nyn lego). He reports they jointly agreed (synomologesat).
limits: This records the reciprocal-procedure frame; it does not assess whether the exchange is equitable or whether Crisias will fulfill the promise.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0021
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 19b-19c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 19b-19c
  start_marker: 19b
  end_marker: 19c
  start_char: 3664
  end_char: 4517
  text_sha256: a4ba5053a90e17f095025e974aa00556c28bcac045cfcb5d5ebdb6f28e6a8031
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates poses a new question at 19b-c: he wants to hear an account of the city they described yesterday engaged in motion, specifically competing against other cities in war and diplomatic speech, performing deeds appropriate to its education and upbringing."
textual_basis: At 19b Socrates introduces a new request (akouoit' an ede ta meta tauta peri tes politeias) and elaborates it with an extended analogy about wanting to see beautiful animals in motion and contest (19b-19c).
limits: This sets up the dramatic frame but does not guarantee that the subsequent speeches will deliver exactly what Socrates requests.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0022
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 19b-19c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 19b-19c
  start_marker: 19b
  end_marker: 19c
  start_char: 3664
  end_char: 4517
  text_sha256: a4ba5053a90e17f095025e974aa00556c28bcac045cfcb5d5ebdb6f28e6a8031
greek_terms:
  - ζῷα
  - γραφῆς
  - ζῶντα
  - ἀγωνίαν
  - ἀθλοῦντα
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates uses a visual-art analogy at 19b to describe his desire. He compares the static city-in-speech to painted or living animals at rest, and expresses the wish to see them in motion and competitive struggle (agonian).
textual_basis: The analogy at 19b contrasts animals produced by painting (hypo graphes eirgasmena) with truly living ones (zonta alethinos) and then pivots to the desire to see them moving and competing (kinoumena ... kata ten agonian athlounta). Socrates applies the same feeling (tauton kai ego pepontha) to the city described earlier.
limits: This is a single analogy used as a dramatic transition, not a developed craft analogy in the argumentative sense. It does not here develop into a theory of mimesis.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0023

observation: Socrates directly addresses Critias and Hermocrates at 19c, naming them as the recipients of his request to hear the city-in-speech described in action.
textual_basis: At 19c Socrates says taut' oun, o Kritia kai Hermokrates, directly naming both interlocutors as addressees of his request.
limits: This establishes the dramatic addressees of the upcoming speeches but does not itself characterize Critias and Hermocrates beyond their names.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0024
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 20e-21a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

  end_marker: 21a
  start_char: 6940
  end_char: 7690
  text_sha256: eb35e8b6a0dc3fcc73ac4ba50475303cf6f68a6d930934e5597965f4157822f4
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Critias reports a logos that passed from Solon through a chain of kin: Solon was related to Dropides, and spoke to Critias the elder (the grandfather of the speaking Critias), who repeated it to the younger generation."
textual_basis: At 20e, Critias says Solon was oikeios and a close friend of Dropides the great-grandfather; the story was told to Critias the grandfather, who in turn recounted it to the younger generation (20e-21a).
limits: This observation records the transmission chain as stated by the character Critias. It does not assess the historicity of the chain or whether Socrates endorses it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates asks Critias what the unnamed great and wondrous ancient deed of the city is — a deed not talked about (ou legomenon) but actually performed (praxthen ontos) according to Solon's report.
textual_basis: "At 21a, Socrates presses Critias: 'But what deed is this, Critias, not spoken of, but actually done by this city in ancient times as Solon reported it?' The direct question comes after Critias mentions a megiston deed in need of commemoration."
limits: This observation identifies the Socratic turn that launches the Atlantis narrative. It does not address whether the deed is historical or legendary.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Critias frames the telling of the ancient deed as an act of gratitude (apodounai kharin) to Socrates and a fitting hymn of praise (humnountas enkomiazein) to the goddess at the festival (paneguris).
textual_basis: At 20e-21a, Critias says that recalling the greatest of the city's ancient deeds would be suitable for repaying thanks to Socrates and for justly and truly praising the goddess at the festival. The occasion is the Panathenaea (implied by the festival context earlier in the framing).
limits: This observation marks the piety frame for the Atlantis story. It does not claim the story is genuinely pious or that the piety is ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 21d Socrates asks Critias to speak from the beginning and explicitly requests the what, how, and from-whom of Solon's true-heard report.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0028
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 21d-21e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

  text_sha256: a3225dfa851bd05bd427ea97a343514bb4df71aa5e7f2c2e8542f3def60c36d2
greek_terms:
  - μεγίστης
  - ὀνομαστοτάτης
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates asks Critias what the story (logos) was. Critias responds that it concerned a deed 'of the greatest and most renowned and most justly so of all deeds' that Athens once performed but whose account has not survived to the present time due to time and the destruction of those who performed it. Socrates then asks Critias to relate it from the beginning.
textual_basis: When Socrates asks 'what was the story, Critias?' (21d), Critias replies that it was 'about the greatest and most renowned of all, most justly so, deed — which this city once performed but the account has not endured to the present time due to time and the destruction of those who performed it' (21d). Socrates then asks him to tell it from the beginning (21d). This sets the dialogue's dramatic subject as the recovery of a lost Athenian deed.
limits: This observation records the dramatic program being set. It does not address whether the account is fictitious or the historical import of the claim. The content of the deed is not yet specified in this segment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0029

  end_char: 9480

greek_terms:
  - Σαϊτικὸς
  - Σάις
  - Νηίθ
  - Ἀθηνᾶ
  - φιλαθήναιοι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Critias locates the origin of the story in the Egyptian district of Sais, where the goddess worshipped is Neith in Egyptian but identified as Athena in Greek according to the Egyptians' own account. The people of Sais are described as particularly friendly to Athens (philathenaioi) and claim a kinship with Athenians.
textual_basis: "Critias specifies at 21e: the Saitic nome in the Delta where the Nile divides, with Sais as its greatest city — home of King Amasis — and the city's founding goddess Neith, called Athena in Greek by the account of the Egyptians, who are 'greatly friendly to Athenians and claim to be in some way akin to them.' The cultural syncretism between the Egyptian goddess Neith and the Greek Athena is explicitly marked."
limits: This observation records the prosopographic and mythic-geographic framing. It does not assess the historical validity of the Egyptian-Athenian kinship claim or the goddess identification. The account of the deed itself has not yet been delivered.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 20c Socrates says he has come prepared to receive in return (antapodosein) the gifts of speeches (ta ton logon xenia) from his interlocutors, establishing a reciprocal obligation that structures the dialogue's dramatic setup.

review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 20d Hermocrates says that Critias brought in a story from ancient hearsay (ek palaias akoes) and asks him to tell it now so that Socrates can judge its suitability for the assigned task. This embeds a reported narrative within the dialogue frame.
limits: This record identifies only the frame-depth device of a reported narrative from old hearsay. It does not evaluate the truth status of the story or its content.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - λόγον
  - ἀπετέλεσεν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Egyptian origin of the Atlantis story is first introduced in an embedded recollection by old Critias at a rhapsody contest during the Apaturia. At 21c, old Critias states that Solon brought a logos from Egypt and would have completed it had he not been forced by civil strife and other troubles to abandon it.
textual_basis: At 21c, old Critias refers to the logos that Solon 'brought here from Egypt' and says Solon failed to complete it because of civil strife (staseis) and other troubles encountered on his return. Within the present span, this Egyptian logos is introduced inside Critias the elder's speech at the Apaturia festival, which Critias the younger is now recounting in the dialogue.
limits: This observation records the Egyptian provenance claim as it first appears in the embedded recollection at 21b-21c. It does not evaluate whether the claim is historically accurate or whether the embedding signals fictionality.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - Ἕλληνες ἀεὶ παῖδές ἐστε
  - γέρων δὲ Ἕλλην οὐκ ἔστιν
english_gloss: Greeks are always children; there is no old Greek; you are all young in your souls.
observation: The Egyptian priest addresses Solon and through him all Greeks, characterizing Greeks collectively as perpetual children in soul, lacking ancient transmitted opinion or time-whitened learning.
textual_basis: At 22b the priest says that Greeks are always children and that there is no old Greek, and elaborates that they have no ancient heard opinion or learning grown hoary with time.
limits: This observation records a prosopographic characterization of a collective ethical type (Greeks as a people) by a named speaker within the dialogue; it does not assert that Plato endorses the priest's view.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0035

  end_char: 10340
  text_sha256: 073f53a35c163d25cdbdb534d6947884cad1c7c0174929e72813656e8f0db8cb
greek_terms:
  - Φορωνέως
  - Νιόβης
  - Δευκαλίωνος
  - Πύρρας
english_gloss: Phoroneus, Niobe, Deucalion, Pyrrha — Greek mythic genealogical figures cited as the most ancient topics Solon could recount.
observation: The Egyptian priest reports that Solon, when prompted to speak about ancient matters, attempted to recount the oldest Greek traditions by mythologizing about Phoroneus (called the first), Niobe, and after the flood about Deucalion and Pyrrha, then genealogized their descendants and tried to count the years.
textual_basis: At 22a-b, Solon is described as attempting to speak of Phoroneus the first-named, Niobe, and after the cataclysm Deucalion and Pyrrha, mythologizing about how they survived, genealogizing their descendants, and attempting to compute the chronology by number of years.
limits: This record identifies the enumeration of mythic figures as a narrative move within a reported dialogue; it makes no claim about the historical truth of these figures or about Plato's own view of Greek myth.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - παλαιὰν δόξαν
english_gloss: ancient hearing, old opinion, learning hoary with time
observation: "The priest's speech at 22b embeds a chain of oral transmission concepts: the Greeks lack ancient hearing and therefore lack old opinion and time-whitened learning, contrasting Egyptian institutional memory with Greek cultural amnesia through the reported words of a named Egyptian priest."
textual_basis: At 22b the priest's reported speech diagnoses Greek ignorance as the absence of ancient hearing producing old opinion and learning made hoary by time, citing a mechanism of intergenerational oral transmission that the priest's own tradition possesses.
limits: This record notes the reported-speech framing and the transmission-chain vocabulary; it does not claim that Plato endorses the Egyptian priest's diagnosis of Greek culture.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: celestial stream, disease, without writing, without music/education, like young people
observation: The Egyptian priest describes a recurring celestial stream that comes upon newly equipped cities like a disease and leaves the Greeks without writing or education, so they begin again like young people ignorant of old events.
textual_basis: At 23a-23b the priest says that after cities have recently acquired writing and civic equipment, the usual cycle brings a celestial stream like a disease, leaving the survivors without writing and education and making them begin again as young.
limits: This record captures the post-cataclysm memory-reset mechanism. It does not include the preceding Nile/topography rationale, the temple-archive claim except as nearby context, or any judgment about the literal truth of the cycle.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: Critias anchors the Atlantis narrative to a known empirical reference point by naming the Pillars of Heracles as the mouth of the strait beyond which the Atlantic island lay.
textual_basis: At 24e Critias reports that a great island lay before the mouth which the Greeks call the Pillars of Heracles. The parenthetical address to the interlocutors (hos phate, hymeis, "as you say, you") marks this as a shared contemporary landmark, linking the mythic geography to the actual Mediterranean exit.
limits: This observation identifies the textual device of anchoring myth in known geography. It does not address whether Plato believed the story, nor its truth-value.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0044

greek_terms:
  - ἀληθινὸν
  - πέλαγος ὄντως
  - ἀληθῶς
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Critias constructs a reality hierarchy by contrasting the Mediterranean with the Atlantic: what lies within the strait is a mere harbor with a narrow entrance, while the ocean beyond is truly sea and the land surrounding it is wholly truly continent."
textual_basis: At 25a Critias says what is inside the mouth appears to be a harbor having a narrow entrance, while that sea beyond is truly sea (pelagos ontos) and the land enclosing it would be most correctly called wholly truly continent (pantelos alethos ... epeiros). The triple insistence on truth/being words marks a reality contrast between the familiar and the realm of the story.
limits: This observation identifies a contrastive reality-marking device within the mythic frame. It does not claim that Plato endorses or subverts the reality claims made in the passage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - δύναμις βασιλέων
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Atlantis island is introduced as the site of a great and wondrous coalition of kings whose dominion extended beyond the island itself to many other islands and parts of the continent, using geographic scale claims to establish the magnitude of the mythic power.
textual_basis: At 25a Critias states that in this island of Atlantis a great and wondrous power of kings was established, ruling the whole island, many other islands, and parts of the continent. The combination of size language (megale) and wonder language (thaumaste) with geographic sweep (the whole island plus many other islands and continental parts) constructs the empire's scale as a mythic datum.
limits: This observation identifies a narrative device for establishing mythic magnitude. It does not address the historicity of Atlantis or the political valence of the empire description.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At the 24c-d boundary the text explicitly states "since the goddess is both warlike and philosophic" as the explanatory reason for why she selected the site that would bear men most similar to her.
```

```yaml

  - φρονιμωτάτους ἄνδρας
english_gloss: ""
observation: The goddess selects Athens as a settlement site because the seasonal climate (eukrasia ton horon) would produce the most prudent men (phronimotatous andras) at 24c.
textual_basis: At 24c the text says the goddess "selected the place in which you were born, having observed the good temperament of the seasons in it, because it would bear most prudent men." This is a causal link from climate to human character.
limits: This observation records a climate-to-character causal claim within the mythic narrative; it does not assert that the dialogue endorses environmental determinism as a doctrine or that this passage establishes a systematic theory.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: The ancient Athenians' surpassing virtue is explained by their status as "offspring and nurslings of gods" (gennemata kai paideumata theon) at 24d.
textual_basis: At 24d the text states that the ancient Athenians surpassed all humans in every virtue, "as is fitting for those who are offspring and nurslings of gods." Divine descent and rearing are offered as the explanation for their superlative virtue.
limits: This observation records that divine pedigree is invoked to explain civic virtue within the mythic narrative of ancient Athens; it does not claim that this reflects a general anthropological thesis in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0049
transient: true
source_work: Timaeus

  end_char: 13587
  text_sha256: c699daabf67ba3d27f95cabe1bbe303202b232f9c5be116323479547ec368634
greek_terms:
  - φθόνος οὐδείς
english_gloss: no envy / no grudge
observation: |
  The Egyptian priest at Sais disclaims any envy before narrating ancient Athenian history to Solon, then gives three positive motives: for Solon's sake, for Athens's sake, and especially for the goddess who founded both cities.
textual_basis: |
  At 23d the priest prefaces his account with a denial of phthonos and follows it with three stated reasons for speaking (for Solon, for Athens, for the goddess). This turns the embedded report of the Atlantis story into an act of deliberate goodwill rather than a grudging or competitive disclosure.
limits: |
  Does not establish that the priest is reliable or that the Atlantis narrative is factual; only records the framing device.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: |
  earlier by a thousand years ... eight thousand years ... nine thousand years
observation: |
  The priest embeds a precise temporal framework in the Atlantis story: Athens was founded a thousand years before Sais, Egyptian sacred records go back eight thousand years, and the ancient Athenians being discussed lived nine thousand years ago.
textual_basis: |
  At 23d-23e the priest states Athens was founded a thousand years earlier than Sais, that Egyptian sacred writings record eight thousand years, and that he will speak of citizens from nine thousand years ago.
limits: |
  Records the chronological claim as a narrative framing device; does not assess whether Plato intends it as literal or mythical dating.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - δῦσα ἠφανίσθη
  - ἄπορον
  - ἀδιερεύνητον
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 25d, the Atlantis destruction is narrated as a bounded catastrophic event: a hard day and night arrive, the whole Athenian fighting force sinks into the earth, Atlantis sinks into the sea and disappears, and the sea remains impassable and unexplorable because of mud.
textual_basis: |
  The sentence is structured around the parallel sinking of the Athenian force and Atlantis, then gives the present condition of the sea as the consequence.
limits: |
  This records the bounded destruction narrative at 25d. It does not include the following transmission-frame transition, assess the account's historicity, or claim that the whole Atlantis story functions as myth in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: |
  The phrase at 25d-25e identifies the source as the ancient Critias (tou palaiou Kritiou) and the mode of transmission as hearing from Solon (kat' akoen ten Solonos). This is a named, multi-link chain: Solon to Critias the elder to the present narration.
limits: |
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0054

greek_terms:
  - χθὲς
  - περὶ πολιτείας
  - δαιμονίως
  - τύχης
  - Σόλων
english_gloss: ""
observation: Critias says that Socrates' previous-day discourse about the city and its men reminded him of the Atlantis story, because much of Socrates' account coincided with Solon's account by a divine-seeming fortune.
textual_basis: At 25e, after summarizing the account received through the elder Critias from Solon, Critias explicitly connects his recollection to Socrates' speech on the previous day about a city and its men, and he marks the convergence with Solon's account as not missing the mark by some divine-seeming fortune.
limits: This records the dramatic occasion for introducing the Atlantis narrative. It does not claim the coincidence is historically genuine, and it does not decide whether the prior discourse corresponds to another Platonic work.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0055
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 26a-26b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 26a-26b
  start_marker: 26a
  end_marker: 26b
  start_char: 17521
  end_char: 18302
  text_sha256: d7abd76585eb17c7d09eec471de4c2bae3406e0fee11564c6e7b4d7116b573f5
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: The speaker (Critias) reports that he could not immediately recount the story from the previous day because he had not sufficiently remembered it after a lapse of time (dia chronou gar ouch hikanos ememnemen, 26a). He then describes a process of first rehearsing everything adequately to himself (pros emauton proton hikanos panta analabonta, 26a) and subsequently reconstructing the account overnight (apelthon te schedon ti panta episkopon tes nuktos anelabon, 26b). He remarks that what he heard only yesterday he may not be able to recall entirely, but astonishes that anything he heard long ago (pampolyn chronon, 26b) could have escaped him.
textual_basis: At 26a Critias explains he did not speak immediately because he could not sufficiently remember after a lapse of time; at 26a-26b he narrates the process of private rehearsal then overnight reconstruction; at 26b he explicitly contrasts his unreliable recent memory with his reliable remote memory.
limits: This observation records a memory disclaimer within the frame narrative; it does not assess whether Critias is truthful or whether this hedge signals unreliability of the embedded Atlantis account.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0056
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 26a-26b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 26a-26b
  start_marker: 26a
  end_marker: 26b
  start_char: 17521
  end_char: 18302
  text_sha256: d7abd76585eb17c7d09eec471de4c2bae3406e0fee11564c6e7b4d7116b573f5
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Critias characterizes the task of providing a logos suitable to the intentions (logon tina prepona tois boulemasin hypothesthai, 26a) as the greatest work (megiston ergon) in all such undertakings. He also cites the proverbial saying ta paidon mathemata thaumaston echei ti mnemeion (26b) to comment on the lasting power of childhood learning as an explanation for his memory of the remote past.
textual_basis: At 26a Critias offers a methodological reflection on what constitutes the greatest task; at 26b he invokes a proverb (to legomenon) about childhood learning having a remarkable memory monument, which serves as a stylistic gloss on his own narrative capacity.
limits: This observation records self-conscious narrative commentary within the frame; it does not evaluate the truth or adequacy of Critias's subsequent account.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0057
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 26a-26b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 26a-26b
  start_marker: 26a
  end_marker: 26b
  start_char: 17521
  end_char: 18302
  text_sha256: d7abd76585eb17c7d09eec471de4c2bae3406e0fee11564c6e7b4d7116b573f5
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Critias narrates a multi-stage transmission sequence: he agreed quickly to the task assigned the previous day (26a), then on departing he rehearsed the story to himself and reported to his companions (pros tousde anepheron auta anamimneskomenos, 26b), and afterward spent the night reviewing nearly everything (apelthon te schedon ti panta episkopon tes nuktos anelabon, 26b). The segment embeds a second-order narrative frame in which the speaker reconstructs a prior conversation from the previous day's agreement."
textual_basis: "At 26a-26b Critias describes a temporal sequence: yesterday's agreement, immediate departure and rehearsal with companions, overnight review. This establishes a layered narrative frame where the present speech is the product of a remembered and reconstructed prior agreement."
limits: This observation notes the frame mechanics of narrative reconstruction; it does not assess the fidelity of the reconstructed content or the historical reality of the Atlantis story.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: Socrates marks a distinction between a fabricated myth and a true account, characterizing the forthcoming narrative of the ideal city as not fiction but genuine discourse.
textual_basis: At 26e, Socrates says the account should not be a fabricated myth but a true logos, drawing an explicit boundary between what is being undertaken and myth understood as fiction.
limits: This observation records the myth-logos contrast at the transition into the Timaeus discourse. It does not evaluate whether the subsequent cosmology itself contains mythic elements or satisfies the standard of truth Socrates invokes.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: most skilled in astronomy, especially knowledgeable about the nature of the universe, beginning from the generation of the cosmos
observation: Critias identifies Timaeus as the most accomplished astronomer among them and as having made the nature of the universe his chief study, then assigns him the first speech starting from the generation of the cosmos.
textual_basis: At 27a, Critias describes Timaeus as astronomikotaton (most skilled in astronomy) and as having made it his work to know the nature of the whole, and prescribes that he speak first, beginning from the generation of the cosmos.
limits: This observation notes the character profile and speech assignment without assessing whether the subsequent discourse reflects astronomical expertise or whether the identification is accurate.
```

```yaml

  - τελευτᾶν δὲ εἰς ἀνθρώπων φύσιν

observation: "Critias announces the speaking order: Timaeus will speak first, beginning from the generation of the cosmos and ending at human nature; Critias will speak second, having received from Timaeus the account of humans generated in speech and from Socrates those humans specially educated."
textual_basis: At 27a, Critias explicitly assigns the first speech to Timaeus and gives himself the second slot, while also specifying the content arc from cosmic generation to human nature.
limits: This records the procedural management of speaking turns. It does not assess whether the announced order is followed or whether the content boundaries are honored in the subsequent discourse.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  The Egyptian priest describes the geographic extent of the Atlantean empire using four specific boundary markers: Libya up to Egypt on the south, and Europe up to Tyrrhenia on the north (25b).
textual_basis: |
  The text at 25b explicitly maps the empire's reach with named territories (Libya, Egypt, Europe, Tyrrhenia) as cardinal geographic limits, using the formula 'up to' (mechri) to indicate the extent of rule.
```

```yaml

  At 25c the text uses 'later' (hysteroi de chronoi) to mark a temporal shift and introduces 'excessive earthquakes and floods' (seismon exaisiōn kai kataklysmōn) as the agents of destruction.
```

```yaml

textual_basis: At 27b Socrates indicates his own speech has been delivered and at 27c he asks Timaeus to speak next; the request is framed as a reciprocal exchange of logoi. Socrates' own turn is described as complete and the transition to Timaeus is marked.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0064

textual_basis: "At 27c Timaeus states that all who have even a small share of moderation call upon a god at the start of every undertaking, and then applies this to the present speech: those about to give an account of the universe must call upon gods and goddesses in prayer. This explicitly announces a procedural rule before the argument begins."
limits: This observation only notes the stated procedural norm; it does not evaluate whether the norm is followed consistently throughout the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 28b, Timaeus states that the first thing to examine about the universe is whether it always was, having no beginning of generation, or came to be, starting from some beginning.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0066

  - ὁρατὸς
  - ἁπτός
  - αἰσθητά
  - γιγνόμενα
english_gloss: ""
observation: The text infers that the cosmos came to be from its being visible and tangible, on the grounds that all such sensible things are grasped by opinion with perception and have been shown to be generated.
textual_basis: At 28b-c, the answer that the cosmos came to be is supported by the claim that it is visible, tangible, and has body; all such things are sensible, and sensible things, grasped by opinion with perception, have been shown to be coming to be and generated.
limits: This observation records the inference from perceptibility to generatedness at 28b-c. It does not assess whether the inference is valid or whether the premise about sensible things is defended elsewhere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

textual_basis: At 28c, Timaeus asserts that for what comes to be, it is necessary that it comes to be through some cause.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0068
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 29a-29b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 29a-29b
  start_marker: 29a
  end_marker: 29b
  start_char: 22347
  end_char: 23188
  text_sha256: c37c03af820b4e611e8ce5fc547aa865e44b6975421a6d82c7b1cf0aef50ad67
greek_terms:
  - δημιουργὸς
  - ἀίδιον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The passage frames the cosmos's creation as a craftsman deciding between two possible models: the eternal and unchanging or something that has come to be. It argues that since the cosmos is beautiful and the craftsman is good, he must have looked to the eternal model."
textual_basis: "At 29a, the craftsman is introduced within a disjunctive question: did he look to the eternal or the created? The argument then uses the premise that the craftsman is good to conclude he looked to the eternal. The term for paradigm appears at 29b in the phrase about the paradigm of the image."
limits: This observation records the presence of craft-analogy structure and the model-copy framework. It does not assess whether the craftsman is a literal or figurative agent, nor does it interpret the theological implications.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0069
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 29a-29b

greek_terms:
  - εἰκόνα
  - παραδείγματος
english_gloss: ""
observation: The passage asserts that the cosmos is necessarily an image of something (29b). It then pairs the concepts of image and paradigm as the governing distinction for what follows.
textual_basis: "At 29b, the conclusion from the preceding craft-analogy argument is drawn: the cosmos must be an image of something. The terms for image and paradigm are then introduced as the organizing distinction for the methodological discussion that follows."
limits: This records the image-paradigm distinction as stated. It does not claim that this is equivalent to the form-particular distinction in other dialogues, nor does it interpret what the paradigm is.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0070
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 29a-29b

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

  end_char: 23188
  text_sha256: c37c03af820b4e611e8ce5fc547aa865e44b6975421a6d82c7b1cf0aef50ad67
greek_terms:
  - λόγους
  - συγγενεῖς
  - εἰκόνος
  - παραδείγματος
  - ἀνελέγκτοις
  - ἀνικήτοις
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 29b, the text articulates a methodological principle that accounts should be akin to their subject matter. Accounts of what is stable, firm, and grasped by intellect should themselves be stable and irrefutable.
textual_basis: At 29b, the passage states that a determination must be made about image and paradigm, on the grounds that accounts are akin to the things they expound. It then specifies that accounts of the stable should be stable, unchangeable, irrefutable, and invincible — insofar as this is possible.
limits: This records the methodological principle as stated. It does not assess whether Timaeus consistently follows this principle. The likely-account corollary is not fully visible in this segment cut.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0071
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 27d-28a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 27d-28a
  start_marker: 27d
  end_marker: 28a
  start_char: 20792
  end_char: 21554
  text_sha256: d8618985eb113fe76d2f105a0ee092af6757f8b5d9c99c910dc870fcd959691a
greek_terms:
  - τὰ μὲν περὶ θεῶν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Timaeus marks an explicit boundary at 27d between the preceding invocation concerning the gods and the speaker's own forthcoming exposition, signaling a shift from divine/theological address to human rational discourse.
textual_basis: At 27d, Timaeus says 'let the things about gods have been called upon in this way; but our own part must be called upon' (with the Greek terms listed in greek_terms), then immediately introduces a methodological distinction between being and becoming. This explicitly separates the preceding divine content from what follows.
limits: This observation notes the boundary marker but does not assert that what follows is free of mythic or theological content. The passage only marks a transition in register, not a complete exclusion of divine topics.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0072
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 27d-28a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 27d-28a
  start_marker: 27d
  end_marker: 28a
  start_char: 20792
  end_char: 21554
  text_sha256: d8618985eb113fe76d2f105a0ee092af6757f8b5d9c99c910dc870fcd959691a
greek_terms:
  - τὸ ὂν ἀεί, γένεσιν δὲ οὐκ ἔχον
  - νοήσει μετὰ λόγου
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 27d-28a, Timaeus introduces a foundational ontological distinction between what always is and has no becoming, and what is always becoming but never is, each paired with a distinct mode of cognition: the former is grasped by intellection with reason, the latter by opinion with irrational sense-perception."
textual_basis: Timaeus at 27d-28a divides all things into (1) what is always, having no becoming, grasped by intellection with reason (noesis meta logou), always being in the same respect; and (2) what is always becoming, never being, grasped by opinion with irrational sense-perception (doxa met' aistheseos alogou), coming to be and perishing, never really being.
limits: This observation records the distinction as stated. It does not claim that this is a theory of Forms identical to that in the Phaedo or Republic, nor does it assess whether this two-level ontology is consistently maintained throughout the Timaeus.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0073
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 27d-28a

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 27d-28a

  end_char: 21554
  text_sha256: d8618985eb113fe76d2f105a0ee092af6757f8b5d9c99c910dc870fcd959691a
greek_terms:
  - δημιουργὸς
  - πρὸς τὸ κατὰ ταὐτὰ ἔχον βλέπων
  - παραδείγματι
  - ἰδέαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 28a, Timaeus introduces the figure of the craftsman (demiourgos) who produces something by looking to what is always the same as a paradigm, and states that when the craftsman uses such an eternal model the result is necessarily beautiful.
textual_basis: Timaeus at 28a states that whenever the craftsman looks to what is always in the same state, using such a paradigm, and brings to completion its form and power (idea kai dynamis), the result is necessarily beautiful of necessity (kalon ex anankes).
limits: This observation identifies the introduction of the craft analogy in the cosmological preamble. It does not evaluate whether the demiurge is a literal creator or a figurative device, nor does it address how this analogy is developed in later passages.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0074
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 30b-30c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 30b-30c
  start_marker: 30b
  end_marker: 30c
  start_char: 24584
  end_char: 25448
  text_sha256: 556b98401ae3968ab6b9a7e81e7007fff52fdebcd21f81b0ef0a6724df7c0c54
greek_terms:
  - κατὰ λόγον τὸν εἰκότα
english_gloss: ""
observation: The speaker explicitly marks the account as a "likely account" (eikōs logos) at 30b-c, signaling that what follows is not demonstrative truth but the most probable account available.
textual_basis: At the close of the reasoning about the cosmos as an ensouled intelligent living creature (30b-c), the speaker inserts the formula "according to the likely account" (kata logon ton eikota) — thereby framing the whole preceding construction (intellect in soul, soul in body) as an eikōs logos rather than a necessary demonstration.
limits: This observation does not assess whether other passages in Timaeus use similar hedges, nor does it determine the epistemological status of the likely account in relation to the forms. It only notes the explicit demarcation formula.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0075
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 30b-30c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 30b-30c
  start_marker: 30b

  end_char: 25448

greek_terms:
  - συνετεκταίνετο
  - ἀπειργασμένος
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The demiurge's activity in 30b-c is described with craft-construction vocabulary: he composes, builds together, and aims at a finished work that is most beautiful and best."
textual_basis: At 30b, the demiurge is said to place intellect in soul and soul in body, composing the whole together (the verb synistas and synetektaineto are used). The goal is producing the most beautiful and best finished work (ergon apeirgasmenos). At 30c the agent is called "ho synistas" (the composer/constructor).
limits: This observation identifies the craft register of the passage but does not specify which particular craft (carpentry, weaving, etc.) the vocabulary evokes. It does not compare this craft language with other passages in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0076
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 30b-30c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 30b-30c
  start_marker: 30b
  end_marker: 30c
  start_char: 24584
  end_char: 25448
  text_sha256: 556b98401ae3968ab6b9a7e81e7007fff52fdebcd21f81b0ef0a6724df7c0c54
greek_terms:
  - τίνι τῶν ζῴων αὐτὸν εἰς ὁμοιότητα
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 30c, the speaker identifies the model for the cosmos as the intelligible living creature which contains all other intelligible living things within itself, rejecting as models any particular living creatures that exist in the form of a part.
textual_basis: At 30c, the speaker asks which living creature the cosmos was made in likeness of, rejects particular creatures (those existing in the form of a part) because nothing incomplete can be beautiful, and posits instead that the cosmos is most like that creature of which the other living things are parts, individually and by kinds — identified as the intelligible living creature which encompasses all intelligible living things in itself.
limits: This observation does not evaluate whether this intelligible paradigm is a Platonic Form in the strict sense, nor does it compare this model with the Form of the Good or other Forms in other dialogues.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0077
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 31b-31c

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

greek_terms:
  - μονογενὴς
  - οὐρανὸς
english_gloss: ""
observation: Timaeus asserts that the maker produced not two or infinitely many worlds, but exactly one unique heaven, and that it has come to be and will continue to be.
textual_basis: At 31b, the text states explicitly that the maker made neither two nor infinitely many kosmoi, but one only-begotten heaven, grounded in the model-copy relationship with the all-perfect living creature.
limits: This records the uniqueness claim. It does not assess whether the argument from the model-copy relation is valid, nor does it address later passages where unity may be qualified.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0078
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 31b-31c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 31b-31c
  start_marker: 31b
  end_marker: 31c
  start_char: 26082
  end_char: 26780
  text_sha256: 227fb309184e71df7ec652a9c144c644dd5d3b8f4f8b7dc8e2d55a35da1f676b
greek_terms:
  - δεῖ
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The text presents a necessity-based chain linking the sensible qualities of the generated cosmos-body to specific elements: visibility requires fire, tangibility requires something solid, and solidity requires earth. The god therefore began composing the body of the all from fire and earth."
textual_basis: At 31b, the term for 'must' introduces the requirement that the generated thing be bodily, visible, and tangible. Then a linked series of negations establishes that no visibility without fire, no tangibility without solid, no solid without earth.
limits: This records the chain form of the deduction. It does not evaluate its physical correctness or completeness, nor does it address whether other elements are later introduced.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0079
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 31b-31c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 31b-31c
  start_marker: 31b
  end_marker: 31c

  end_char: 26780
  text_sha256: 227fb309184e71df7ec652a9c144c644dd5d3b8f4f8b7dc8e2d55a35da1f676b
greek_terms:
  - δεσμὸν
  - δεσμῶν
  - ἀναλογία
english_gloss: ""
observation: Timaeus states a mathematical principle that two things alone cannot be well combined without a third, that a bond must come between them. The finest bond is that which makes itself and the bound things maximally one, and this is achieved by proportion.
textual_basis: At 31c, the text says two alone cannot be well combined without a third. It proceeds to identify the finest bond as that which makes unity, and this is naturally accomplished by proportion.
limits: This records the geometric bonding principle. It does not assess whether the reasoning applies the principle correctly to cosmic composition, nor does it resolve what the third term will be (this unfolds in later passages).
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0080
source_work: Timaeus

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 32c-33a

  end_char: 28574
  text_sha256: 30dc6ccfdd6609211c9be18b4ca4d833cbcd070766cfad6d5236905831fdbecf
greek_terms:
  - σύστασις
  - συνέστησεν
  - συνιστάς
  - συνδήσαντος
  - διανοηθείς
english_gloss: ""
observation: The passage clusters explicit construction vocabulary around the demiurge's act of composing the cosmic body, presenting the cosmos as a crafted artifact whose maker reasons about design purposes before executing them.
textual_basis: At 32c the cosmos body comes together into sameness with itself and is bound (language of binding and coming-together); the composition of the cosmos took each of the four elements as a whole; at 32c-d the one who composed it put it together; at 32d the demiurge reasoned these things through before enumerating the purposes of the design.
limits: This observation notes only the clustering of construction and reasoning terms; it does not claim the craft analogy is the organizing framework of the entire Timaeus or that the demiurge is presented as a literal craftsman rather than a figurative constructor.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0081
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 32c-33a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 32c-33a
  start_marker: 32c
  end_marker: 33a
  start_char: 27702
  end_char: 28574
  text_sha256: 30dc6ccfdd6609211c9be18b4ca4d833cbcd070766cfad6d5236905831fdbecf
greek_terms:
  - ἵνα
  - ἀγήρων
  - ἄνοσον
english_gloss: ""
observation: The demiurge's decision to use all of each of the four elements is justified by a chain of three purpose clauses that build a cumulative argument moving from completeness to unity to imperishability.
textual_basis: "At 32d-33a, three purpose clauses are marked by 'first, so that...' and 'further, so that...': (1) that the cosmos be a whole complete living being from complete parts, (2) that it be one since nothing was left out from which another such thing could arise, (3) that it be ageless and without sickness. A causal explanation follows at 33a: external forces dissolve composite bodies and bring disease and old age."
limits: This observation notes the syntactic structure of the purpose chain; it does not evaluate whether the teleological reasoning is valid or whether it reflects Plato's own view rather than Timaeus's character speech.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0082
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 32a-32b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 32a-32b
  start_marker: 32a
  end_marker: 32b
  start_char: 26780
  end_char: 27702
  text_sha256: aa75df2e02aea311ddc1f97cbaa38f69572e73dad4369016a66dc035f39f8d84
greek_terms:
  - μεσότης
  - ἓν
  - ἐξ ἀνάγκης
english_gloss: ""
observation: The text presents a formal mathematical principle that when a middle term stands in continuous proportion to extremes, all terms collapse into identity and unity, and this principle is then applied to the binding of the cosmic body through elemental means.
textual_basis: At 32a the text states that if the middle is such that the first is to it as it is to the last, then the middle becomes first and last and the extremes become middles, so all are necessarily the same and all will be one. At 32b this proportion principle is applied when the god places water and air as means between fire and earth and works out their proportional ratios, binding the cosmos into a unified whole.
limits: This observation records the cosmogonic use of mathematical proportion. It does not evaluate the mathematical validity of the argument or claim that Timaeus invented the proportion principle.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0083
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 32a-32b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 32a-32b
  start_marker: 32a
  end_marker: 32b
  start_char: 26780
  end_char: 27702
  text_sha256: aa75df2e02aea311ddc1f97cbaa38f69572e73dad4369016a66dc035f39f8d84
greek_terms:
  - μεσότητες
  - ἐπίπεδον
  - βάθος
english_gloss: ""
observation: The text argues that the solidity of the cosmic body necessitates two intermediate elements rather than one, because solids are always bound by two means whereas a plane body would need only one mean. This geometric constraint directly determines the number of elements in the cosmos.
textual_basis: "At 32a-32b the text presents a conditional: if the body of the universe needed to be a plane without depth, one mean would suffice to bind it; but since it must be solid, and solids are always fitted together by two means, never one, two means are required. This geometric reasoning explains why four elements (fire, earth, water, air) are needed rather than three."
limits: This observation records the geometric constraint argument linking solidity to the number of elemental means. It does not assess whether the geometric claim about solids is mathematically correct or whether it originates with Plato.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0084

  - συνέδησεν

  - ὁ θεὸς
  - ἀπεργασάμενος
english_gloss: ""
observation: The god is portrayed as a craftsman who actively places the elements in proportional relation, works out their ratios, and binds them together into the visible and tangible heaven.
textual_basis: At 32b the god places water and air in the middle between fire and earth, works them into the same proportional ratio as far as possible, and binds and constitutes the visible and tangible heaven. The verbs of placing, working out, binding, and constituting are drawn from the domain of craft and construction.
limits: This observation records the craft-like language applied to divine cosmogonic action. It does not assert that the passage is an analogy rather than a literal account, or that the demiurge is a mere metaphor.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0085
source_work: Timaeus

  - ἐτορνεύσατο
  - ἀπηκριβοῦτο
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The demiurge is described using specific craft verbs: he turned the cosmos on a lathe and made its surface precise all around."
textual_basis: At 33b the cosmos is described as circular, turned on a lathe, and at 33c the exterior is made smooth and precise. Both verbs belong to the domain of skilled manual craftsmanship.
limits: This observation registers the presence of craft-language verbs applied to the demiurge; it does not claim that the entire Timaeus cosmology is reducible to a craft analogy or that the demiurge is merely a craftsman.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0086
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 33b-33c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 33b-33c
  start_marker: 33b
  end_marker: 33c
  start_char: 28574
  end_char: 29410
  text_sha256: b528ac06ee525292cefa40be2a5c1ebdf9dcd4f347e38052d74d0cd3570747fe
greek_terms:
  - πρέπον
  - συγγενές
  - σφαιροειδές
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The spherical shape is chosen for the cosmos through a fittingness argument: the shape given is the fitting and the akin. Because the cosmos is to contain all living beings within it, the fitting shape is the one that contains all shapes — the sphere. Likeness is judged far better than unlikeness."
textual_basis: At 33b, the fitting-and-akin premise introduces the shape choice. The reasoning chain moves from the cosmos as container of all living beings to the sphere as the shape that contains all shapes, concluding with the spherical designation. The aesthetic-teleological principle that likeness is far better than unlikeness closes the argument.
limits: This observation identifies the structure of the fittingness argument; it does not assess whether the argument is valid, nor does it claim this is the only mode of reasoning in the Timaeus.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0087
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 33c-33d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 33c-33d
  start_marker: 33c
  end_marker: 33d
  start_char: 28966
  end_char: 29679
  text_sha256: 0ada3a3bf28955986bec3a8bfc060dfb3ef19cce7fc48d34f83c68f9b88c9b9a
greek_terms:
  - ὀμμάτων
  - ἀκοῆς
  - ἀναπνοῆς
  - τροφὴν
  - φθίσιν
  - αὔταρκες
  - προσδεὲς
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The cosmos is described as needing nothing external: it lacks eyes, hearing, respiration, and nutritive organs because no external visible, audible, breathable, incoming, or outgoing object remains for them. It nourishes itself from its own decay and is judged better as self-sufficient than as needing others."
textual_basis: At 33c-33d, each denied organ is paired with the absence of an external correlate. The passage adds that nothing departs from or approaches the cosmos, that it provides nourishment to itself from its own decay, and that the maker judged it better as self-sufficient than needing others.
limits: This observation registers the self-sufficiency claim and its negative argument structure. It does not address the prior spherical-shape argument or the later removal of hands and feet.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0088
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 34b-34c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 34b-34c
  start_marker: 34b
  end_marker: 34c
  start_char: 30101
  end_char: 30913
  text_sha256: 265ad66ce033d8f8417132deffb349b3a0a2878d407fbc96f7f2f504edb6bb5e
greek_terms:
  - προτέραν
  - πρεσβυτέραν
  - δεσπότιν
  - ἄρξουσαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Timaeus explains that the soul is prior and older than the body in both generation and excellence because it is set up as mistress and ruler over what is to be ruled.
textual_basis: At 34c, Timaeus corrects the order of his exposition by saying the god did not make soul later or younger than body. He then states that soul is prior and older than body in generation and excellence, as mistress and ruler over what is to be ruled.
limits: This records a soul-body priority claim grounded in rule language. It does not treat the passage as a craft analogy, and it does not settle broader questions about the status of the world soul.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0089
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 36c-36d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

  start_marker: 36c
  end_marker: 36d
  start_char: 32757
  end_char: 33620
  text_sha256: 3243f26ed93cbbcc663a20895e704e42890fa38d6629b0a3b00211fb7b1111e3
greek_terms:
  - συνάψας
  - ἐποιεῖτο
  - σχίσας
  - προσέταξεν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Demiurge is described as actively constructing the world-soul through a sequence of craft-like operations: joining the circles together, making the outer and inner circles, splitting the inner circle, and ordering the circles to move in specific directions."
textual_basis: At 36c-36d, the Demiurge joins the circles into one, makes outer and inner circles, splits the inner circle sixfold, and orders them to move in contrary directions. The verbs depict a craftsman producing and arranging material.
limits: This observation notes the language of craft-production applied to soul-construction; it does not assess whether the Demiurge is literally a craftsman or whether the account is metaphorical.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - διπλασίου
  - τριπλασίου
  - ἑπτὰ κύκλους
  - ἀνίσους
  - ἐν λόγῳ
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Demiurge structures the inner circle of the world-soul according to precise mathematical proportions, splitting it into seven unequal circles arranged by the intervals of the double and triple, with three circles on each side and all moving in rational proportion.
textual_basis: At 36d, Timaeus states that the inner circle was split sixfold into seven unequal circles according to the interval of the double and triple, with three on each side, the circles moving in proportion.
limits: This observation notes the mathematical structuring of the soul; it does not interpret the philosophical significance of the specific ratios or their relationship to the earlier construction of the soul-mixture.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0091
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 35a-35b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 35a-35b
  start_marker: 35a
  end_marker: 35b
  start_char: 30913
  end_char: 31791
  text_sha256: 1281201c89fa09d61ed29ae77bd45b4e082af00ceb0d34c80cf1f429d94a1d23
greek_terms:
  - ἀμερίστου
  - μεριστῆς
  - οὐσίας
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The demiurge constructs a three-tiered ontology of being: indivisible being that is always in the same state, divisible being that comes to be in the realm of bodies, and a third intermediate form of being mixed from both (35a)."
textual_basis: "The text explicitly distinguishes three kinds of being (ousia): (1) the indivisible and always unchanging kind, (2) the divisible kind associated with bodies and becoming, and (3) a third form (triton...ousias eidos) mixed from both, placed in the middle (en mesōi). This is distinct from the later mixing of Same and Different, which are added to the being-mixture."
limits: This observation records the textual layering of being-types. It does not claim that this is a doctrine of forms or that the intermediate being corresponds to soul alone; the passage itself locates the mixing within the soul's construction.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0092

  end_char: 31791
  text_sha256: 1281201c89fa09d61ed29ae77bd45b4e082af00ceb0d34c80cf1f429d94a1d23
greek_terms:
  - δύσμεικτον
  - βίᾳ
  - συναρμόττων
english_gloss: ""
observation: The nature of the Different is described as hard to mix (dysmeikton), and the demiurge fits it together with the Same by force or compulsion (biai, 35a).
textual_basis: The passage states that the nature of the Different is dysmeikton (hard to mix), and the demiurge harmonizes it into the Same by force (eis tauton synarmottōn biai). This casts the mixing as a craft operation on resistant material, not a smooth blending.
limits: This records the craft-like language of forced harmonization. It does not claim that the passage is an allegory for dialectic or that the difficulty maps onto any specific epistemological claim elsewhere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0093
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 35a-35c

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
```
