```yaml
  end_char: 59189
  text_sha256: f57227519b5bf5783e15df5f3ffe7dda2eeebf4d4afbe84d45f845a03db0569c
greek_terms:
  - προαπορηθῆναι
  - πυρὸς
  - ὕδωρ
  - πῦρ
  - πιστῷ
  - βεβαίῳ
  - λόγῳ
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The attempt to clarify the receptacle requires a prior puzzle about elemental identity: it is difficult to say with a trustworthy and stable account which visible condition should be called water, fire, or any one thing."
textual_basis: At 49b, Timaeus says clearer speech about the third kind is difficult because one must first be puzzled about fire and the things with fire. He states the difficulty of saying which one should truly be called water rather than fire, or anything rather than all of them, in a reliable and firm account.
limits: This records the stated problem of naming the elements under change. It does not solve that problem or identify the receptacle by itself.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0180

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

  end_marker: 49c

  end_char: 59597
  text_sha256: aab95a3fed8eb71937960abf9aaa2132d46d3092b29fd1a1fc56cd9538563931
greek_terms:
  - πηγνύμενον
  - τηκόμενον
  - διακρινόμενον
  - πῦρ
  - ἀέρα
  - ὕδωρ
  - γῆν
  - κύκλον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Timaeus gives an elemental transformation sequence: what has just been called water hardens into earth and stones, then the same thing is described as melting and separating into air, burning into fire, returning through air, cloud, mist, flowing water, and again earth and stones."
textual_basis: At 49b-49c, Timaeus says what is now named water is seen hardening into stones and earth, melting and separating into air, burning into fire, condensing back through air, cloud, and mist into flowing water, and again becoming earth and stones. The sequence is explicitly called a cycle of generation into one another.
limits: This records the transformation sequence as the text presents it. It does not establish a modern physical account or fix the elements as permanent substances.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0181
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 49d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 49d
  start_marker: 49d
  end_marker: 49d
  start_char: 59597
  end_char: 59989
  text_sha256: 02584fe9f5bb71ead6e83023c45df105a9d2040f688f935dd3e21a8864027944
greek_terms:
  - φανταζομένων
  - τοιοῦτον
  - πῦρ
  - ὕδωρ
  - βεβαιότητα
english_gloss: ""
observation: Because the elemental appearances never show themselves as the same, Timaeus says they should not be named as stable items, but only as such-and-such cases like fire or water.
textual_basis: "At 49d, he asks how anyone could confidently assert that any one of these is this and not another when each never appears as the same. He then states the safer way to speak: call what is observed becoming differently not this fire, but what is such as fire, and likewise for water and other cases."
limits: This records the proposed naming discipline for changing appearances. It does not deny that the appearances occur.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0182
source_work: Timaeus

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 49e-50a

  end_marker: 50a
  start_char: 59989
  end_char: 60832
  text_sha256: 94e97f5b0ba79b4bdbd121d3bf137fe8658db8fa92dfce6dce9776252aa98588
greek_terms:
  - τόδε
  - τοῦτο
  - μόνιμα
  - τοιοῦτον
  - γένεσιν
  - ἐγγιγνόμενα
  - φαντάζεται
  - προσχρωμένους
english_gloss: ""
observation: The passage reserves deictic naming for the field in which generated appearances come to be and perish, while denying that generated qualities can be indicated as stable beings.
textual_basis: At 49e-50a, Timaeus says generated things flee terms that indicate them as permanent, including deictic expressions. Each generated appearance should instead be called the recurring such-and-such, while only that in which each appears and from which it perishes should be addressed with this and this-here names.
limits: This records the contrast between naming generated appearances and naming their receiving field. It does not yet define the receiving field beyond its role in appearances.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0183

  - θερμὸν
  - λευκὸν
  - ἐναντίων
  - χρυσοῦ
  - μεταπλάττων
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Timaeus begins a clarifying analogy: qualities such as hot, white, and opposites should not be identified with the receiving field, just as changing shapes made from gold invite a question about what the item really is."
textual_basis: At 50a, he says the receiver should not be called any quality, whether hot, white, an opposite, or anything derived from them. He then introduces the case of someone continually remolding all shapes out of gold and being asked what one of them is.
limits: This records the setup of the analogy. The marker begins but does not complete the answer supplied by the next span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0184
source_work: Timaeus

  - χρυσός
  - τρίγωνον
  - σχήματα
  - δεχομένης
  - σώματα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The gold analogy is completed and applied to the all-receiving nature: the safer answer is the enduring material rather than any transient shape, and the receiver must be called always the same."
textual_basis: At 50b, Timaeus says the safest answer to the changing gold shapes is gold, not triangle or any shape that changes while being posited. He applies the same account to the nature receiving all bodies, saying it should always be spoken of as the same because it never departs from its own power.
limits: This records how the analogy distinguishes enduring receiver from changing shapes. It does not identify the receiver with gold literally.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0185

greek_terms:
  - μορφὴν
  - ἐκμαγεῖον
  - διασχηματιζόμενον
  - μιμήματα
  - τυπωθέντα
english_gloss: ""
observation: The receiver is said never to take on any form like the things entering it; it functions as an impression-bearing field moved and reshaped by incoming imitations.
textual_basis: At 50c, Timaeus says the receiver has never in any way taken on a form resembling any of the things that enter. It is set down as a mold or impression-field for all things, moved and variously shaped by entrants, while the entering and departing things are imitations stamped from beings in a difficult manner.
limits: This records the receiver's formlessness and impression-bearing role. It does not explain the difficult mode of stamping, which the text postpones.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0186
source_work: Timaeus

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

  end_marker: 50d
  start_char: 61662
  end_char: 62060
  text_sha256: c75c62221994e6eab1d1796b5942a78bea87d623e8df8e63968864ab8f4b2310
greek_terms:
  - γιγνόμενον
  - ἐν ᾧ
  - ὅθεν
  - μητρί
  - πατρί
  - ἐκγόνῳ
  - ἄμορφον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Timaeus states a threefold schema for generation: what comes to be, that in which it comes to be, and that after which it is likened; these are compared to offspring, mother, and father."
textual_basis: "At 50d, he says three kinds must be conceived: the becoming thing, the thing in which it becomes, and the source from which the becoming thing grows by likeness. He then compares the receiver to mother, the source to father, and the intermediate nature to offspring, adding that the receiving item must be formless relative to all forms it will receive."
limits: This records the explicit relational schema and family analogy. It does not infer a biological doctrine from the analogy.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0187
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 50e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 50e
  start_marker: 50e
  end_marker: 50e
  start_char: 62060
  end_char: 62609
  text_sha256: 15b0bd8f669d33f659f8e36c487e900f06729950509b0cf81cd842ab3025065a
greek_terms:
  - ἐκτὸς εἰδῶν
  - ἐκδεξόμενον
  - ἀώδη
  - ὀσμάς
  - σχήματα
  - λειότατον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The receiver's lack of its own form is justified by comparison with odorless liquids and smoothed materials: to receive all forms well, it must not already display one of the incoming characters."
textual_basis: At 50e, Timaeus says that if the receiver resembled any entrant, it would receive contrary or wholly different natures badly by showing its own look alongside them. He therefore says what will receive all kinds must be outside all forms, and compares this with making scent-bearing liquids as odorless as possible and preparing soft materials as smooth as possible before stamping shapes into them.
limits: This records the formless-receiver requirement and its examples. It does not claim the receiver is literally a liquid or craft material.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ὑποδοχὴν
  - ἀνόρατον
  - ἄμορφον
  - πανδεχές
english_gloss: ""
observation: The conclusion from the receiver examples is that the mother and receptacle of the visible generated world should not be called earth, air, fire, water, or their derivatives, but invisible, formless, and all-receiving.
textual_basis: At 51a, Timaeus says what will receive likenesses of all eternal beings must be by nature outside all forms. He then says not to call the mother and receptacle of the visible and sensible generated thing earth, air, fire, water, what comes from them, or what they come from, but rather an invisible, formless, all-receiving kind.
limits: This records the negative and positive naming instruction for the receptacle. It does not identify it with any one element.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0189
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 51b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

greek_terms:
  - πεπυρωμένον
  - ὑγρανθὲν
  - μιμήματα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Elemental names are redirected to conditions of the receiver: fire is the fiery part, water the moistened part, and earth and air appear insofar as the receiver accepts their likenesses; this leads into a renewed question about things themselves."
textual_basis: At 51b, Timaeus says the fiery part of the receiver appears as fire, the moistened part as water, and earth and air appear insofar as it receives imitations of them. He then asks whether there is fire itself by itself and likewise the things always spoken of as themselves by themselves.
limits: This records the shift from elemental appearances in the receiver to the question about self-standing objects. It does not yet answer the question.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0190
source_work: Timaeus

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

  end_marker: 51c

  end_char: 63832
  text_sha256: 2a0dc7f7b167c8d55e4aea0726035c417a99c2c32d414caa338fffa2dbf858b8
greek_terms:
  - εἶδος
  - νοητόν
  - λόγος
  - ἄκριτον
  - ἀδίκαστον
  - διισχυριζόμενον
english_gloss: ""
observation: Timaeus frames the issue as an alternative between intelligible forms for each thing and the view that only bodily perceptibles have truth, but refuses to leave the present question undecided while asserting one side dogmatically.
textual_basis: At 51c, he asks whether the things seen and otherwise sensed through the body alone have this sort of truth, with no intelligible form of each thing beyond them and form-language being mere speech. He then says it is not worthy to leave the present matter unjudged and undecided while strongly asserting that this is how matters stand.
limits: This records the alternative and the refusal of an unjudged assertion. It does not yet state Timaeus' vote, which follows in the next marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0191
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 51d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

  text_sha256: d804813222fe72f62c95b2e40ef5620a5b8edec1c5855991d63492d88592ccd9
greek_terms:
  - ὅρος
  - ψῆφον
  - νοῦς
  - δόξα ἀληθής
  - εἴδη
  - νοούμενα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Timaeus offers a brief criterion for forms: if intellect and true opinion are two kinds, then things themselves exist apart, imperceptible to us and apprehended only by intellect."
textual_basis: "At 51d, he says he will cast his own vote. If intellect and true opinion are two kinds, then these things exist entirely by themselves: forms imperceptible to us and thought only. If true opinion differs in no way from intellect, then all bodily sensibles must be set down as most secure."
limits: This records the conditional criterion and Timaeus' framing of it. It does not by itself list the later differentiators between intellect and opinion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0192

greek_terms:
  - διδαχῆς
  - πειθοῦς
  - ἀληθοῦς λόγου
  - ἄλογον
  - ἀκίνητον
  - μεταπειστόν
  - νοῦ
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Timaeus distinguishes intellect and true opinion by origin, account, stability, and distribution: one comes through teaching with true account and remains unmoved by persuasion, while the other comes through persuasion, is without account, and is changeable."
textual_basis: At 51e, he says the two must be spoken of as separate because they arise separately and are unlike. One comes through teaching, the other through persuasion; one is always with true account, the other without account; one is unmoved by persuasion, the other alterable by persuasion; all humans share in true opinion, while gods and only a small human group share in intellect.
limits: This records the stated contrast between the two cognitive kinds. It does not infer who belongs to the small human group.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0193

  end_char: 65034
  text_sha256: 7fb5550b77c114c4c5b0643211d08b0a9494022bf1f555c66359271e9fee42d2
greek_terms:
  - εἶδος
  - ἀγέννητον
  - ἀνώλεθρον
  - αἰσθητόν
  - γεννητόν
  - τόπῳ
  - χώρας
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The previous distinctions are summarized into three: an ungenerated and imperishable form grasped by thought, a sensible generated likeness that comes to be in a place and perishes, and the always-existing kind of space."
textual_basis: At 52a, Timaeus says one thing has the same form, is ungenerated and imperishable, receives nothing into itself, enters nothing else, and is invisible and insensible, belonging to thought's inspection. A second is homonymous and like it, sensible, generated, always carried, coming to be in some place and perishing from there. A third kind is the always existing space.
limits: This records the threefold summary. It does not collapse the form, copy, and space into one another.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ἕδραν
  - μόγις πιστόν
  - ὀνειροπολοῦμεν
  - τόπῳ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Space is described as the seat for all generated things, hard to trust and grasped without sense-perception by a kind of bastard reasoning, while ordinary thought dreams that everything that is must be in some place.
textual_basis: At 52b, the third kind gives a seat to all things that have generation and is touched without sense-perception by a certain bastard reasoning, being barely credible. Looking toward it, Timaeus says, people dream that every being must be somewhere in a place and occupy some space, and that what is neither on earth nor in heaven is nothing.
limits: This records the epistemic difficulty and place-claim attached to space. It does not endorse the dreamlike claim as fully true.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: "The dreamlike pressure about place is applied to images: because an image is a phantom of something else and not its own ground, the account says it should come to be in another or else be nothing."
textual_basis: At 52b-52c, Timaeus says the receptacle is grasped by a bastard reasoning and is connected with a dreamlike insistence that being must be somewhere. This dreamlike condition prevents clear speech about the sleepless nature, and the image is then described as a phantom of another that must come to be in something else while clinging somehow to being, or else be nothing at all.
limits: This records the image-dependence argument in the marker. It does not supply a full theory of images beyond the stated dependence.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - γενέσεως

observation: "Timaeus gives the threefold result in compact form: being, space, and becoming are three distinct items and are already in place before the heaven comes to be."
textual_basis: At 52d, he says that, according to his reckoning, the summary account is that being, space, and becoming are, three in three ways, before the generation of heaven. He then resumes with the nurse of generation undergoing moistening, burning, and receiving the shapes of earth and air.
limits: This records the compact summary and transition back to the nurse of generation. It does not yet describe the full motion and sorting process that follows.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - διακρινόμενα
english_gloss: ""
observation: The nurse of generation is described as visually varied and unstable because unequal, unbalanced powers fill it; it is shaken by them and, in moving, shakes them back.
textual_basis: At 52e, Timaeus says the nurse appears manifold because it is filled with neither similar nor balanced powers, so no part of it is in equilibrium. It is shaken irregularly everywhere by those powers, and when moved it in turn shakes them; the things moved are always carried in different directions and separated.
limits: This records the instability and reciprocal shaking stated in the marker. It does not include the continuation of the winnowing comparison beyond 52e.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - μέγιστον
  - ὀξύτατον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "After assigning the cube to earth, Timaeus ranks the remaining elemental forms by mobility, size, and sharpness: water receives the least mobile of the remaining shapes, fire the most mobile and smallest, and air the middle position."
textual_basis: At 56a the likely account is preserved by allotting one form to earth, then assigning the least-mobile remaining form to water, the most-mobile form to fire, and the middle form to air. The same marker ranks the bodies by size and sharpness, placing fire as smallest and sharpest, water as largest, and air between them.
limits: This records the ranking used for the elemental assignment. It does not yet name the corresponding regular solids, which is completed in the next marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 56b the account says that the body generated as a pyramid is to be the element and seed of fire, that the second in generation should be called air, and that the third should be called water.
limits: This records the named elemental assignments. It does not add a separate account of how the solids later break apart or recombine.
```

```yaml

  - ἀνὰ λόγον
english_gloss: ""
observation: Timaeus adds that individual elemental particles are too small to be seen, but their bulk becomes visible when many gather together, and he ties their numbers, motions, and powers to divine proportional arrangement wherever necessity yielded.
textual_basis: At 56c each single body of each kind is said to be invisible because of smallness, while the masses of many gathered particles are visible. The same marker says god harmonized the proportions of their numbers, motions, and powers as precisely as necessity's persuaded nature allowed.
limits: This records the visibility and proportional-ordering claims. It does not specify the subsequent transformations among earth, fire, water, and air.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0201

  - πυρὶ
  - ὀξύτητος
  - συναρμοσθέντα
  - ὕδωρ
  - μερισθέν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Earth is treated as a non-converting body in the transformation account: fire may dissolve and carry its parts in fire, air, or water, but when those parts meet and fit back together they become earth again because earth would not pass into another form."
textual_basis: At 56d, after marking the account as likely, Timaeus says earth meeting fire is dissolved by fire's sharpness and carried until its parts meet and reassemble with themselves; he explicitly adds that it would never come into another form. The same marker then begins a contrasting account of water divided by fire or air.
limits: This records earth's restricted transformability and the start of the contrast with water. It does not complete the water conversion ratios, which continue into the next marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0202

english_gloss: ""
observation: "The conversion ratios among the three non-earth bodies are worked out in reverse directions: an air segment can make two fire bodies, defeated fire can combine two bodies into one air form, and defeated air can be compacted from two and a half wholes into one water form."
textual_basis: At 56e, Timaeus says pieces of air from one dissolved part would become two fire bodies. He then describes small fire enclosed by air, waters, or earth, defeated in motion, and broken down so that two fire bodies combine into one air form; when air is overcome and divided, two and a half wholes compact into one whole water form.
limits: This records the stated conversion arithmetic among fire, air, and water. It does not include earth as intertransformable with them.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0203
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 57a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 57a
  start_marker: 57a
  end_marker: 57a
  start_char: 74840
  end_char: 75259
  text_sha256: 44bff12f1c7cc443c56675245f6c4b6e858887af2c115afabc089fdb9b552b75
greek_terms:
  - γωνιῶν
  - πλευρὰς
  - ὀξύτητι
  - ὅμοιον
  - μεταβολὴν
  - μάχηται
  - λυόμενον
english_gloss: ""
observation: Elemental transformation is treated as a contest that stops once the cut material has assembled into the cutter's nature; like does not alter or suffer from like, while a weaker body becoming something else keeps dissolving as long as it fights a stronger one.
textual_basis: At 57a, Timaeus says a kind caught in fire is cut by the sharpness of fire's angles and sides, but when it has come together into fire's own nature it stops being cut. He explains this with the rule that each like and same kind cannot cause change in, or suffer from, what has the same condition, whereas a weaker thing fighting a stronger one while becoming something else does not stop being dissolved.
limits: This records the stated stopping condition for transformation. It does not identify every later outcome of the contest.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0204
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 57b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 57b
  start_marker: 57b
  end_marker: 57b
  start_char: 75259
  end_char: 75649
  text_sha256: 4ea5f649336d195222e14bb4e2730dd5e6b59742d453f2e912d7a2d85972b09d
greek_terms:
  - κατασβεννύηται
  - κρατοῦντος ἰδέαν
  - πυρὸς
  - ἀήρ
  - ὕδωρ
  - συγγενές
  - σύνοικον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The same stability rule is applied to smaller bodies crowded by larger ones: if they take the ruling form, extinction stops and fire becomes air or air becomes water; if they keep fighting other kinds, they either escape toward kindred bodies or remain with the victor after becoming like it."
textual_basis: At 57b, Timaeus says small bodies enclosed among many larger ones stop being extinguished when they are willing to assemble into the victor's form, producing air from fire and water from air. If they go into the same place and fight some incoming different kind, dissolution continues until they either flee after being thrust apart and dissolved or are defeated and remain as one thing like the conqueror.
limits: This records the two possible endpoints of the transformation contest. It does not claim that the text gives a mechanical example for every possible encounter.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0205

  - τόπον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Place exchange follows likeness change: each kind's mass has its own place because of the receiver's motion, and things that become unlike themselves but like others are shaken toward the place of the kind they now resemble."
textual_basis: At 57c, Timaeus says that all things exchange places according to these affections. The masses of each kind stand apart in their own place because of the motion of the receiver, while things that become unlike themselves and like others are carried by shaking toward the place of those to which they have become similar.
limits: This records the sorting mechanism by likeness and place. It does not infer a fixed geography beyond the marker's own statement about each kind's place.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0206

greek_terms:
  - τρίγωνον
  - ἐλάττω
  - μείζω
  - εἴδεσι γένη
  - συμμειγνύμενα
  - ποικιλίαν
  - εἰκότι λόγῳ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Internal variety within elemental kinds is attributed to the original use of triangles in multiple sizes rather than one size for each kind; their self-mixing and mutual mixing make the variety unlimited, and the likely account of nature requires attending to it.
textual_basis: At 57d, Timaeus says the cause of other kinds being implanted within the elemental forms is that each triangle was not planted at the beginning with only one size, but with smaller and larger sizes in as many numbers as there are kinds within the forms. Because these mix with themselves and with one another, their variety is unlimited, and those who will use a likely account about nature must become observers of it.
limits: This records the stated source of sub-elemental variety. It does not enumerate the varieties themselves.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0207
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 57e-58a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 57e-58a
  start_marker: 57e
  end_marker: 58a
  start_char: 76536
  end_char: 77330
  text_sha256: c997c173973b1cfa10659715eded4112061f29b50eb70459ea58737df6960bba
greek_terms:
  - κινήσεως
  - ὁμαλότητι
  - ἀνωμαλότητα
  - κινησόμενον
  - κινῆσον
  - ἀνισότης
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Before proceeding, Timaeus makes motion and rest explicit: motion is said not to occur in uniformity because mover and moved are both required and cannot be uniform, so rest is assigned to uniformity, motion to irregularity, and inequality to the cause of the irregular nature."
textual_basis: At 57e-58a, Timaeus says the later reasoning needs prior agreement about motion and rest. Motion is not willing to be in uniformity because mover and moved cannot exist apart from each other and cannot ever be uniform; he then places rest in uniformity, motion in irregularity, and names inequality as the cause of the irregular nature.
limits: This records the motion-rest principle. It does not include the following circular-compression account that explains why the separated kinds keep moving through one another.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0208
source_work: Timaeus

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

  end_char: 77330

greek_terms:
  - ἀνισότης
  - περίοδος
  - σφίγγει
  - κενὴν χώραν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Timaeus reopens the question why the separated elemental kinds have not stopped moving through one another and first answers by invoking the circular revolution of the whole, which compresses all things and leaves no empty space.
textual_basis: At 58a, after the motion-rest principle has been completed, Timaeus says the account has not yet explained why the separated kinds have not ceased their movement and passage through one another. He then names the circular revolution of the whole as enclosing the kinds, tightening everything, and leaving no empty place.
limits: This records only the no-void compression setup at 58a. It does not re-record the rest-in-uniformity and motion-in-irregularity principle, and it does not include the following gap-filling mechanics of small and large bodies.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0209
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 58b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 58b
  start_marker: 58b
  end_marker: 58b
  start_char: 77330
  end_char: 77785
  text_sha256: 2d1b2bff9320ff4fb75e1627d8651dea07c25eb3066164bb136d3cef4ae536e3
greek_terms:
  - πῦρ
  - κενότητα
  - διάκενα
  - πιλήσεως
  - συνωθεῖ
  - τόπους
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The compression account is made more mechanical: fire passes through everything most, air next, and bodies made from the largest parts leave the largest gaps, so compression pushes small bodies into the gaps of larger bodies and keeps everything moving toward its own places."
textual_basis: At 58b, Timaeus ranks fire and air by their ability to pass through other bodies, says the largest-part structures leave the largest emptiness in their composition while the smallest leave the least, and explains that compression drives small bodies into the intervals of large ones. The marker then describes small bodies separating larger ones while larger bodies combine the smaller, so all things are carried up and down toward their own places.
limits: This records the gap-and-compression mechanism within the elemental account. It does not identify the later named subtypes of fire, air, or water.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0210

textual_basis: At 58c, the first sentence links change of size with change of place-position, then says that the preserved generation of irregularity provides the motion of these bodies continuously.
limits: This records only the continuing-motion conclusion at 58c. It does not preserve the fire subtype list that begins later in the marker, and it does not duplicate the neighboring air and water subtype enumeration already represented by obs_timaeus_0211.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0211
source_work: Timaeus

  - σκότος
  - τριγώνων
  - ἀνισότητα
  - ὕδατος
  - ὑγρόν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The subtype enumeration continues by distinguishing named and unnamed forms of air from triangle inequality, then dividing water first into wet and fusible kinds; the wet kind is explained as mobile because it shares small, unequal water forms and has an irregular shape.
textual_basis: At 58d, Timaeus calls the clearest air aether, the muddiest air mist and darkness, and also mentions unnamed forms generated through inequality of triangles. He then divides water into wet and fusible kinds and says the wet kind is moving both by itself and by another because it contains small unequal water kinds and because of irregularity and the form of its shape.
limits: This records the taxonomy and stated causes of mobility. It does not include the later account of how fire changes the more stationary water kind.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0212
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 58e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 58e
  start_marker: 58e
  end_marker: 58e
  start_char: 78624
  end_char: 79023
  text_sha256: 48bb4adfca420848712cd62b96c033ce73c01be98c94998171bf57c9664a8247
greek_terms:
  - ὁμαλῶν
  - στασιμώτερον
  - ὁμαλότητα
  - κινήσεως
  - τήκεσθαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: The heavier, more stationary water kind is said to be fixed by uniformity until incoming fire dissolves it, removes its uniformity, makes it share more in motion, and allows nearby air to push it downward; the resulting affections are named melting and flow.
textual_basis: At 58e, Timaeus contrasts water made from large uniform bodies with the mobile wet kind, saying the former is more stationary and heavy because it is fixed by uniformity. When fire enters and dissolves it, it loses uniformity and shares more in motion; once mobile, it is pushed by nearby air and stretched toward earth, receiving the names melting for reduction of masses and flow for the downward stretching.
limits: This records the forward change from fixed water to mobile flow. It does not include the reverse process of cooling and freezing.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0213
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 59a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

  start_marker: 59a
  end_marker: 59a
  start_char: 79023
  end_char: 79473
  text_sha256: 427f54bb89e6be04aa071b1efb6b3d1bfc0d4df6cc112bbc7a9bea509feb7e4d
greek_terms:
  - πυρός
  - σύνοδον
  - ὁμαλότητα
  - ἀνωμαλότητος
  - ψῦξιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The reverse water-state process is defined: when fire departs without entering a void, nearby air pushes the still-mobile wet mass into the seats of fire, mixes it with itself, and lets it regain uniformity; the departure of fire is named cooling, and the coming-together after its departure is named freezing."
textual_basis: At 59a, Timaeus says that when fire falls out from the wet mass, it does not leave into a void, so nearby air presses the still-mobile wet bulk into the places of fire and mixes it with itself. The mass regains uniformity because fire, described as the maker of irregularity, has gone away; Timaeus names the removal of fire cooling and the post-removal coming-together a frozen kind.
limits: This records the naming and mechanism of cooling and freezing. It does not cover the subsequent catalogue of fusible waters and metals.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0214

  end_char: 79923

greek_terms:
  - ὕδατα
  - πέτρας
  - ἀδάμας
  - γῆς
  - σκληρότερον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The account treats metals and hard materials as varieties of fusible water: the finest, smoothest, densest, single-formed kind becomes gold when filtered through rock, while a hard blackened offshoot of gold is called adamant; another near-gold kind is made harder by sharing in a small fine portion of earth."
textual_basis: At 59b, Timaeus turns to the waters previously called fusible, saying that the kind formed from the finest and smoothest parts, made densest, single-formed, bright, and yellow, is gold hardened after filtering through rock. He then calls a hardest blackened offshoot of gold adamant, and describes a near-gold kind with more than one form that receives a small fine part of earth and becomes harder.
limits: This records the material taxonomy within the marker. It does not complete the near-gold material's later name or the later account of its corrosion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: Timaeus names one bright frozen water kind with large internal gaps as copper, and says that when the earth mixed with it separates out again as it ages and becomes visible by itself, it is called rust.
textual_basis: At 59c, Timaeus identifies copper from the bright frozen water kind with internal intervals, then describes the earth mixed into it separating again during aging and becoming visible under the name rust.
limits: This records only the copper and rust item at 59c. It does not include the preceding gold, adamant, or near-gold setup, and it does not include the later methodological aside on likely accounts.
```

```yaml
observation: Timaeus frames the pursuit of likely accounts about becoming as a rest from accounts of what always is, producing blameless pleasure and amounting to a moderate and intelligent form of play.
textual_basis: Across late 59c and the opening of 59d, Timaeus says that one who lays aside accounts of beings that always are for the sake of rest and surveys likely accounts about becoming gains pleasure without regret and would make a moderate, intelligent play in life.
limits: This records only the methodological aside. It does not include the copper and rust taxonomy earlier in 59c, and it does not include the next material case involving water mixed with fire later in 59d.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Timaeus classifies compacted water products by degree of solidification and location: the stronger case is named hail above earth and crystal on earth, while the less complete or half-solid case is named snow above earth and frost on earth."
textual_basis: At 59e, the named products are distinguished by whether the solidifying process is stronger or less complete and by whether the product occurs above earth or on earth.
limits: This records only the named compacted water products at 59e. It does not preserve the process that begins earlier, and it does not include the plant-filtered juice taxonomy that begins at the end of this marker and continues afterward.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0222

  - πίττα
  - κίκι
  - ἔλαιον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The plant-filtered juices are sorted by mixture: many mixed kinds remain unnamed, while four fire-containing and especially transparent kinds receive names. Within this marker, wine is identified by its warming effect on soul with body, and an oily kind is identified by smoothness, visual divisiveness, brightness, shine, and fatty appearance, with pitch, castor oil, oil, and other same-powered cases grouped under it."
textual_basis: At 60a, Timaeus says mixtures make the juices unlike one another, leaving many kinds unnamed, but that four fire-containing transparent kinds have received names. The marker names wine as warming soul with body and describes the oily class through its smooth, sight-separating, bright, shining, fatty appearance before listing pitch, castor oil, oil, and other cases of the same power.
limits: This records the unnamed-many and named-four structure and the first named juice kinds within 60a. It does not complete the two remaining named juice kinds, whose descriptions are not finished in this marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 60b, Timaeus identifies one juice by sweetness around the mouth and gives honey as its especially general name. He then names a foamy kind separated from all juices as dissolving flesh by burning.
limits: This records only the completion of the juice names within 60b. It does not include the earth-kind account that begins after the paragraph break.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0224
source_work: Timaeus

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

  text_sha256: 8d211fcfdc33f25ec65d05134173b1eb6879950a2b860597212d23ed94d0f855
greek_terms:
  - ἀήρ
  - ἔωσεν
  - ἔθλιψεν
  - γῆ
  - ὕδατι
  - πέτρα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Stone formation is explained through air displacement without void: newly generated air rises, nearby air is pushed, and the heavier surrounding air compresses the earth mass into the seats from which the new air rose. Earth compacted with water in this way becomes rock, with equal, smooth, transparent parts marking the fairer kind and the opposite parts marking the less fair kind."
textual_basis: At 60c, Timaeus says the generated air rises to its own place, that no void is left over, and that the nearby air is pushed. The pushed heavier air surrounds the earth bulk, presses it hard, and drives it into the places from which the new air rose; earth compressed by air with water becomes rock. The marker then distinguishes the fairer rock by equal, smooth, transparent parts and the opposite kind by contrast.
limits: This records the rock-generation mechanism and the part-quality contrast within 60c. It does not include the fired-clay and other earth-mixture products that follow.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0225
source_work: Timaeus

english_gloss: ""
observation: "The earth-mixture taxonomy adds fired clay, black stone, and natron: fire's rapid removal of moisture makes the rougher solid called ceramic; when moisture remains, liquid earth cooled through fire becomes a black-colored stone; and one salty semi-solid product is named as cleansing oil and earth."
textual_basis: At 60d, Timaeus says the whole moist part is snatched out by the speed of fire and the resulting rougher kind is called ceramic. He also says that when moisture remains, liquid earth cooled after fire becomes a stone with black color. The same marker then describes salty, semi-solid products soluble again by water and names one as cleansing oil and earth.
limits: This records only the products named or fully described within 60d. It does not complete the second salty product, whose name and sensory setting fall outside this marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0226
source_work: Timaeus

  - πῦρ
  - ἀήρ
  - διακένων
  - ἄλυτον
  - ἄτηκτον
  - ὕδατος
  - τήκει
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The account states an access-based solubility rule for earth masses: fire and air do not melt them because their smaller parts pass through earth's wide intervals without force, while larger water parts force a passage, dissolve earth, and melt it."
textual_basis: At 60e, Timaeus says common bodies from both elements are not dissolved by water but are compacted so as to be dissolved by fire. He explains that fire and air have parts smaller than the intervals in earth's structure and pass through without force, whereas water's larger parts make a forceful passage and melt by dissolving.
limits: This records only the first access-based melting rule within 60e. It does not include the salt completion at the start of the marker or the later differentiated rules for compacted earth, water, and air.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0227
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 61a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

  end_marker: 61a
  start_char: 83285
  end_char: 83729
  text_sha256: 18c79d93dd0f4438f1b54485db2126118f8018ae84996d2c96ad1e130d96e218
greek_terms:
  - ὕδωρ
  - λύει
  - εἴσοδος
  - σύνοδον
  - διαχεῖτον
  - διάκενα
  - τρίγωνα
  - ἀέρα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "A graded dissolution table is given for earth, water, and air: unconstituted earth under force is dissolved only by water, constituted earth only by fire, the strongest water cohesion only by fire, weaker water cohesion by both fire and air, violently constituted air only at the elemental level, and unforced air only by fire. The marker then begins the case of bodies mixed from earth and water."
textual_basis: At 61a, Timaeus states separate rules for earth, water, and air. Earth without settled constitution is dissolved only by water, but settled earth admits only fire. The most violent water gathering is dispersed only by fire, while the weaker one is dispersed by fire and air, with different routes through intervals and triangles. For air, nothing dissolves the violently constituted form except according to the element, while unforced air is melted only by fire.
limits: This records the dissolution rules stated within 61a. It does not complete the account of mixed earth-water bodies, which the marker only begins.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0228
source_work: Timaeus

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

  end_char: 84136
  text_sha256: 7984f78ee716fd012051624dd101b54bce567abb928592dac1ea156e45854eff
greek_terms:
  - ὕδωρ
  - γῆς διάκενα
  - βίᾳ
  - εἴσοδον
  - περιρρέοντα
  - ἄτηκτον
  - τηχθέντι
  - ῥεῖν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Mixed earth-water bodies are again described by access to intervals: while water holds the earth intervals compressed by force, incoming water parts cannot enter and merely flow around the whole mass, leaving it unmelted; fire parts enter the water intervals and alone cause the melted common body to flow."
textual_basis: At 61b, Timaeus says that as long as water holds the earth intervals compressed by force, water approaching from outside has no entrance, flows around the whole bulk, and leaves it unmelted. Fire enters the intervals of the waters and does to water what water does to earth, so fire alone is the cause of flowing when the common body is melted.
limits: This records only the access rule for mixed earth-water bodies within 61b. It does not include the list of lower-water mixtures that begins later in the marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0217
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 61c-61d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 61c-61d
  start_marker: 61c
  end_marker: 61d
  start_char: 84136
  end_char: 84987
  text_sha256: 3cb94045a66d4a96ab32d6a7a312205b173dee5eb6497e4c2f9d1b5e68dc7ef8
greek_terms:
  - παθήματα
  - αἴσθησιν
  - σαρκὸς
  - ψυχῆς
  - ὑποθετέον
  - σῶμα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "After nearly completing the account of bodies varied by shapes, combinations, and transformations, Timaeus shifts to the causes of their affections and marks a methodological dependency: sensation, flesh, the parts around flesh, and the mortal soul are needed for the account, but cannot all be explained at once, so one side must be posited first and returned to later."
textual_basis: At 61c-61d, Timaeus says the varieties produced by shapes, communions, and changes into one another have almost been displayed, and that the causes of their affections must now be made clear. He adds that sensation must always be present in what is being discussed, while the generation of flesh, surrounding bodily parts, and the mortal part of soul has not yet been treated; because neither the sensory affections nor those bodily and psychic topics can be adequately stated apart from the other, one must be hypothesized first and then revisited.
limits: This records the explicit ordering problem and ordered method. It does not supply the later full account of flesh or mortal soul.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0218
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 61e-62a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 61e-62a
  start_marker: 61e
  end_marker: 62a
  start_char: 84987
  end_char: 85664
  text_sha256: 3c0b60d300a289ae90af2d71cd282bc2a319e82c31e3ddd283c0889bd3f1bc8c
greek_terms:
  - ὀξύ
  - πλευρῶν
  - γωνιῶν
  - σμικρότητα
  - φορᾶς
  - τέμνει
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Heat is explained as a sharp bodily affection caused by fire's geometry and motion: thin sides, sharp angles, small particles, and rapid movement make it cut what it meets, divide bodies into small pieces, and thereby give rise to the affection and name now called hot."
textual_basis: At 61e-62a, Timaeus says everyone nearly senses that the affection is sharp, then appeals to the thinness of sides, sharpness of angles, smallness of parts, and speed of motion. Remembering the generation of fire's shape, he says this nature cuts what it encounters, divides our bodies into small pieces, and plausibly supplies the affection and name hot.
limits: This records the proposed cause of heat. It does not include the opposite affection of cold, which is treated separately.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0219
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 62a-62b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 62a-62b
  start_marker: 62a
  end_marker: 62b
  start_char: 85216
  end_char: 86106
  text_sha256: 180f37e51d1993c328b3e66fc1ba494815091a40ed9719d38c99abf1178a1db6
greek_terms:
  - ὑγρῶν
  - συνωθοῦντα
  - νοτερόν
  - ὁμαλότητα
  - πήγνυσιν
  - τρόμος
  - ῥῖγος
  - ψυχρόν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Cold is explained as the opposite bodily process: larger surrounding moist bodies enter, drive out smaller ones, compress the body's wetness, and make what had been irregular and moving become unmoved through uniformity and pressure; the forced contraction fights back, and that struggle and shaking are named trembling, chill, and the cold affection."
textual_basis: At 62a-62b, Timaeus says larger parts among the fluids around the body enter and push out smaller ones, cannot settle into the smaller places, and by compressing our wetness make it unmoved from being irregular and moving, because of uniformity and compression. What is gathered contrary to nature fights back toward the opposite, and to this struggle and shaking the names trembling and chill are assigned, while the whole affection and what acts on it receive the name cold.
limits: This records the compression and shaking account of cold. It does not cover the later heavy-light discussion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0220
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 62b-62c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 62b-62c

  end_marker: 62c
  start_char: 85664
  end_char: 86553
  text_sha256: 9255b4eee1e15f859eb059855fe00802f5190e7d1a710e1de67cb2c5a10fc203
greek_terms:
  - σὰρξ
  - ὑπείκῃ
  - τετραγώνων
  - βάσεων
  - ἀντιτυπώτατον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Hard and soft are defined relationally by resistance to flesh: hard is what our flesh yields to, soft is what yields to flesh, and the same relation applies between bodies generally; resistance is tied to stable footing, especially forms based on square bases and dense compactness."
textual_basis: At 62b-62c, Timaeus says hard is whatever our flesh yields to and soft whatever yields to flesh, and that the relation is likewise measured between things themselves. He adds that what rests on a small base yields, while the form made from square bases stands very firmly and is most resistant, especially when it has come together into the greatest density.
limits: This records the hard-soft definition and its geometric basis. It does not develop the heavy-light problem introduced afterward.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0229
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 62c-63a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 62c-63a
  start_marker: 62c
  end_marker: 63a
  start_char: 86106
  end_char: 87648
  text_sha256: 1a39bc30435e59d4d6a0c59b7adee281597b9842c36a280e4df4d07946bbd97d
greek_terms:
  - βαρὺ
  - κοῦφον
  - κάτω
  - ἄνω
  - σφαιροειδοῦς
  - μέσον
  - ἔσχατα
  - ἀντίπους
english_gloss: heavy, light, down, up, spherical, center, extremes, antipodes
observation: Timaeus opens the heavy-light account by rejecting the supposition that nature divides the whole into two opposed places called down and up; in a spherical whole, equidistant extremes and the center make those opposed names inappropriate, and an observer moving around a central body can call the same part down and up.
textual_basis: At 62c-63a, the text says heavy and light are clearest when examined with down and up, then denies that two natural opposed places divide the whole. The reason given is the spherical shape of the heaven, the equal distance of the extremes from the center, and the example of a circular traveler who can stand at antipodes and apply the same down/up language to the same thing.
limits: This records the rejection of absolute directional places inside 62c-63a. It does not include the weighing examples that explain how heavy and light names arise.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0230
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 63b-63e

greek_terms:

  - ἀνόμοιον
  - βίᾳ
  - βαρὺ
  - κοῦφον
  - συγγενοῦς
  - φέρεται
english_gloss: fire, scales, unlike, by force, heavy, light, kindred, is carried
observation: "The positive heavy-light account is built from weighing examples: a smaller quantity is more easily forced away from its kindred region into an unlike medium, so relative ease of forced displacement supplies the names light and up, while the contrary affection supplies heavy and down."
textual_basis: At 63b-63e, Timaeus imagines removing portions of fire in the fire region, placing them in scales, and forcing fire into unlike air. With one force applied to two quantities, the lesser follows the force more readily than the larger. He then applies the same pattern to earthy bodies on earth and states the general rule that the path toward the kindred makes the moving thing heavy and its destination down, with the contrary cases named otherwise.
limits: This records the stated mechanism for heavy/light naming in 63b-63e. It does not treat heavy and light as absolute properties independent of region, since the passage makes them vary with kind and place.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
limits: This records only the brief smooth/rough cause in 63e-64a. It does not explain the pleasure and pain account introduced after the paragraph break in 64a.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0233
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 64b-64c

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt

  end_char: 90604
  text_sha256: c20e1cec66e9980348171c5dffe5101d88bcf1f83caef2c41d5c667a7ca47caf
greek_terms:
  - εὐκινήτου
  - δυσκινήτου
  - πάθος
  - μόρια
  - φρόνιμον
  - ἀναίσθητον
  - ὄψεως
  - ἀκοῆς
english_gloss: easily moved, hard to move, affection, parts, intelligent part, insensible, sight, hearing
observation: "Timaeus distinguishes sensible from insensible bodily affection by mobility: an easily moved nature passes even a small affection in a circular chain from part to part until it reaches the intelligent part, while a stable nature suffers the affection without moving neighboring parts and so remains insensible."
textual_basis: At 64b-64c, the text recalls the division between easily moved and difficult-to-move nature. The easily moved body communicates the same affection through successive parts until it reports the power of the cause to the intelligent part. The contrary nature is fixed, does not move neighboring parts, and leaves the first affection unmoved in relation to the whole living thing; bones, hairs, and earthy parts are named as examples, while sight and hearing are tied to the mobility of fire and air.
limits: This records the mechanism by which bodily affection becomes sensation. It does not treat every sense separately or give the later pleasure-pain rule.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: nature, violent, affection, painful, pleasant, gently, small, insensible
observation: "Pleasure and pain are defined through the scale and direction of bodily change: a violent, collective affection contrary to nature is painful, a collective return to nature is pleasant, and a gentle small-scale change is insensible."
textual_basis: At 64c-64d, after the distinction between sensible and insensible bodies, Timaeus states the rule to be kept in mind for pleasures and pains. What occurs violently and all together against nature is painful, what returns all together into nature is pleasant, and what occurs gently and little by little is insensible.
limits: This records the general rule for pleasure and pain in the cited span. It does not include the visual example used immediately afterward.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ease, perceptible, pain, pleasure, sight, affections, violent, separation
observation: "Visual affections are presented as highly perceptible yet without pleasure or pain: changes to the visual body happen easily, so cuts, burnings, separations, and reunions yield strong perceptions without violent pain or restorative pleasure."
textual_basis: At 64d-64e, Timaeus says that what occurs with ease is most perceptible but does not share in pain or pleasure. He gives the affections concerning sight as the example, explaining that the visual body suffers divisions and reunions without violence and therefore has great and clear perceptions but no pains in being cut or burned and no pleasures when restored to the same form.
limits: This records the painless perceptibility of visual affections. It does not include the later contrast with bodies made of larger parts, which begins after this span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: departures, emptyings, fillings, insensible, perceptive, pleasant smells, burnings, cuts
observation: "Timaeus distinguishes pleasure and pain outcomes by the timing of emptying and refilling: gradual emptying with sudden filling produces no pain but great pleasure, while sudden alienation with difficult gradual restoration produces the opposite pattern, with pleasant smells and bodily burnings or cuts given as the local examples."
textual_basis: At 65a-65b, the text contrasts cases where departures and emptyings happen little by little but fillings happen all at once and in large scale with cases where alienation is sudden but restoration is small-scale and difficult. The first class is said to be evident in pleasant smells; the opposite class is said to be evident in burnings and cuts of the body.
limits: This records the local timing contrast and examples. It does not replace the broader pleasure-pain rule stated immediately before this span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 65b-65c, the passage says the common affections of the whole body and the names given to the things that act on them have nearly been stated. It then says the affections arising in our particular parts and the causes belonging to the active things should be stated, if possible, and introduces the previously omitted tastes around the tongue.
limits: This is a transition record. It does not itself extract a full causal account of any single taste.
```

```yaml
observation: The taste account begins by locating tastes around the tongue and explaining them through combinations and separations, with roughness and smoothness playing a special role; astringent and austere tastes are then tied to how incoming bodies dry and contract the tongue's small veins.
textual_basis: At 65c-65d, Timaeus says tastes are particular affections around the tongue and that, like many other affections, they arise through combinations and separations while also using roughness and smoothness especially. The first example describes incoming bodies around the tongue's small veins that enter moist soft parts of the flesh, melt earthy parts, contract and dry the veins, and appear more or less rough as astringent or austere.
limits: This records only the opening taste mechanism and the astringent-austere pair. It does not include the later bitter, salty, pungent, sharp, or sweet cases.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Bitter and salty tastes are separated by the degree of cleansing action on the tongue: excessive washing that dissolves the tongue's own nature is called bitter, while a weaker lye-like condition that cleans within measure appears salty and more congenial."
textual_basis: At 65d-65e, the passage describes agents that cleanse and wash off what is around the tongue. When they go beyond measure and attack so as to dissolve the tongue's own nature, the case is named bitter; when they fall short of the lye-like condition and use cleansing in a measured way, they appear salty without rough bitterness.
limits: This records the contrast between bitter and salty in this span. It does not treat saltiness outside the tongue-affection mechanism given here.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Pungent tastes are generated when things share in the mouth's heat, are smoothed and ignited by it, burn back against what heated them, rise by lightness toward the head's sense organs, and cut whatever they strike.
textual_basis: At 65e-66a, Timaeus describes things that commune with the mouth's heat, are smoothed by it, are set on fire together and burn back, are carried upward by lightness toward the head's perceptions, and cut what they encounter. Because of these powers, such things are said to be pungent.
limits: This records the causal sequence for the pungent class in the cited span. It does not include the following sharp or sour mechanism involving narrow veins and bubbles.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: The sharp taste class is explained through decayed, thinned bodies entering narrow veins, stirring earthy and airy parts into circular motion, and producing hollow watery vessels of air; the same mechanism accounts for bubbles, boiling, and fermentation before being named the cause of sharp affections.
textual_basis: At 66a-66b, Timaeus describes bodies thinned by decay entering narrow veins and interacting with earthy parts and suitably proportioned air so that they move around each other and make hollow structures around incoming bodies. The passage then names clear cases as bubbles and earthier rising cases as boiling and fermentation, and calls the cause of these affections sharp.
limits: This record follows the internal mechanism stated in 66a-66b. It does not equate this class with modern acidity or fermentation chemistry.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Sweetness is presented as the opposite affection from an opposite cause: a fitting moist composition smooths what has been roughened, gathers or relaxes what is unnaturally compacted or dispersed, restores the tongue as much as possible to nature, and functions as a remedy for violent affections."
textual_basis: At 66c, Timaeus says the opposite affection to the preceding tastes comes from an opposite cause. When the incoming composition in moist things is naturally akin to the tongue's condition, it smooths the roughened parts, gathers some things and loosens others, establishes everything as far as possible according to nature, and is called sweet as a pleasant remedy for violent affections.
limits: This records the sweet-taste mechanism within 66c. It does not infer a general medical theory beyond the stated remedy language.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 66d, Timaeus says there are no species around the power of the nostrils because the whole class of smells is half-formed and no kind has the proportion for having a smell. He adds that the relevant veins are narrower for earth and water kinds and wider for fire and air kinds, and that people perceive smell only in things becoming wetted, decayed, melted, or smoked.
limits: This records only the opening of the smell account in 66d. The final causal explanation continues after this marker and is not included here.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Odor is explained as an intermediate state between water and air: all odors are either smoke or mist, thinner than water and thicker than air, and their perceptibility is shown by blocked breathing, where breath passes without any odor being filtered through."
textual_basis: "At 66e-67a, Timaeus says all odors have arisen in the transition from water to air or air to water, and identifies them as smoke or mist. Mist is what goes from air to water, smoke what goes from water to air, so odors are thinner than water and thicker than air. He adds the breathing test: when something blocks respiration and someone draws breath by force, no odor is filtered along with it, but breath alone follows."
limits: This records the physical account of odors and the breathing test. It does not include the preceding examples of named smells before 66e.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 67a, after the physical account of odors, Timaeus says these varieties are unnamed and do not come from many or simple forms. He says only two are clear there, the pleasant and the painful: one roughens and forces the whole cavity between the head and navel, while the other soothes the same region and gladly restores it toward the way it naturally is."
```

```yaml
observation: "Voice and hearing are defined through a transmitted impact: voice is the blow conveyed through ears by air, brain, and blood as far as the soul, and hearing is the motion produced by that impact from the head down toward the seat of the liver."
textual_basis: At 67b, Timaeus introduces the third sensory part, hearing, and says its affections must be explained. He sets down voice as a blow through the ears, by air, brain, and blood, transmitted until the soul; the motion caused by it begins from the head and ends around the seat of the liver, and this motion is called hearing.
limits: This records the general mechanism for voice and hearing. It does not include the following classification of sound qualities.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Auditory qualities are sorted by features of the motion: fast motion is sharp, slower motion is lower, similar motion is even and smooth, contrary motion is rough, much motion is large, and little motion is small, while harmonies are explicitly deferred."
textual_basis: At 67b-67c, after defining voice and hearing, Timaeus classifies the motion by speed, likeness, texture, and quantity. The fast part is sharp and the slower part lower; the similar is even and smooth and the opposite rough; the large is the much motion and the opposite is small. He then says the matters concerning their harmonies must be stated later.
limits: This records the local taxonomy of sound affections. It does not state the later account of concords or harmonies.
```

```yaml
  text_sha256: c39e21b610c90ea5d75e1d53a77140171eeaa7b1ae1dcc6daac4ade97efd3485
greek_terms:
  - τέταρτον
  - γένος
  - αἰσθητικόν
  - χρόας
  - φλόγα
  - ἀπορρέουσαν
  - ὄψει
  - σύμμετρα
english_gloss: fourth, kind, sensory, colors, flame, flowing off, sight, commensurate
observation: "Color is introduced as a fourth sensory kind with many internal varieties: colors are collectively named as a flame flowing off from bodies, made of particles proportionate to sight for perception."
textual_basis: At 67c, Timaeus turns from hearing to the remaining fourth sensory kind, which must be divided because it has many varieties. He says all of these have been called colors and defines them as flame flowing off from each body, possessing parts proportionate to sight for perception.
limits: This records only the transition and initial definition of color. It does not include the detailed division of colors that follows after this marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: colors, sight, smaller, larger, equal, insensible, transparent, contracting, separating, white, black
observation: "Color perception is explained by the size relation between effluences and the parts of sight: equal particles are insensible and transparent, while larger and smaller particles contract or separate sight and produce the white-black pair as the same kind of affection in a different sensory genus."
textual_basis: At 67d-67e, the account says particles arriving at sight are smaller, larger, or equal to the parts of sight. The equal ones are called insensible and transparent, while the larger and smaller ones act by contracting or separating sight and are grouped with hot-cold bodily affections and rough or pungent tongue affections before the white and black pair is named.
limits: This records the general visual mechanism and the white-black pair. It does not include the later bright, red, or mixed-color cases.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: separating, white, black, sharper, motion, kind of fire, eyes, tear, flashes, bright, shining
observation: Brightness and flashing are explained as a sharper fire-like motion striking sight, forcing and melting the eye passages, producing tears, and generating varied colors through the meeting of outgoing and incoming fire around the eye's moisture.
textual_basis: At 67e-68a, after naming the sight-separating affection white and its opposite black, the account introduces a sharper motion of another fire kind. This motion separates sight up to the eyes, forces and melts the passages, pours out fire and water called tears, and makes varied colors in the resulting mixture; the affection is named flashing and its producer bright and shining.
limits: This records the bright and flashing mechanism. It does not treat red or the later blended colors as separate cases.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: fire, moist, mixed, red, yellow, measure, necessity, likely account, moderately
observation: "The color-mixture account withholds exact quantitative proportions: after naming red and yellow from mixtures involving fire, eye moisture, bright, red, and white, Timaeus says that specifying how much of each ingredient belongs with each would be pointless even for someone who knew it, because neither necessity nor a moderate likely account can be supplied."
textual_basis: At 68b, the account explains red through a fire kind mixed with the eye's moisture and yellow through bright mixed with red and white. It then interrupts the mixture list to deny that exact measures and proportions should be stated, saying no necessity or adequate likely account could be given for them.
limits: This records the methodological limit on exact ratios within the color account. It does not deny the named mixture relations that the passage continues to list.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Timaeus presents named colors as products in a mixture sequence: after naming red and yellow through mixtures, the account continues through purple, dark, fiery-red, grey, pale, deep blue, blue-grey, and leek-green as further mixture products."
textual_basis: Across 68b-68c, the passage says exact measures would be irrational to state, then gives a named sequence in which colors arise from mixing, burning, blackening, brightening, and further combinations.
limits: This records the named mixture sequence only. It does not supply exact proportions or extend the rule to colors not named in the span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0253
source_work: Timaeus

  - διασῴζοι

  - βάσανον
  - ἀνθρωπίνης
  - θείας
  - συγκεραννύναι
  - διαλύειν
english_gloss: others, mixtures, preserve, likely myth, in practice, test, human, divine, mix together, dissolve
observation: "The remaining color account is explicitly bounded as a likely account rather than an experimentally testable recipe: other colors may be assimilated to the listed mixtures if that preserves the likely myth, but testing the account in practice would ignore the difference between divine and human capacities to combine many into one and dissolve one into many."
textual_basis: At 68d, Timaeus says the other colors are nearly clear from the preceding mixtures if the account preserves the likely myth. He then warns that someone who seeks a practical test of these things would fail to know the difference between human and divine nature, because god can adequately mix many into one and dissolve one into many, while no human can do either now or later.
limits: This records the stated methodological boundary on the color-mixture account. It does not claim that the color list is arbitrary or that the passage rejects all empirical observation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: from necessity, craftsman, most beautiful, best, self-sufficient, most complete, causes, serving, crafting well
observation: "Necessary conditions are subordinated to the divine craftsman's ordering role: Timaeus says the maker of the most beautiful and best used these necessary causes as servants while himself crafting the good in generated things."
textual_basis: At 68e, after the color account, Timaeus says that all these things were naturally this way from necessity when the craftsman generated the self-sufficient and most complete god. The craftsman is described as using the causes around these things as serving causes while himself designing the good in everything generated.
limits: This records the auxiliary role assigned to necessary causes in this transition. It does not identify every previous physical process with a single cause, and it does not replace the next explicit division between necessary and divine causes.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0255

greek_terms:
  - δύ’ αἰτίας
  - ἀναγκαῖον
  - θεῖον
  - εὐδαίμονος βίου
  - φύσις
  - ἐνδέχεται
  - κατανοεῖν
  - λαβεῖν
  - μετασχεῖν
english_gloss: two causes, necessary, divine, happy life, nature, admits, understand, grasp, partake
observation: "Timaeus states an explicit two-cause rule: divine causes should be sought in all things for the acquisition of a happy life as far as human nature permits, while necessary causes should be studied for the sake of the divine causes because the desired objects cannot be understood, grasped, or shared in without them."
textual_basis: At 68e-69a, the discourse distinguishes two kinds of causes, the necessary and the divine. It directs inquiry toward the divine for the sake of a happy life, within the limits of human nature, and toward the necessary for the sake of those divine causes because without the necessary causes the things being pursued cannot be understood, obtained, or otherwise participated in.
limits: This records the methodological division and priority relation between divine and necessary causes. It does not specify the later physiological account that follows from the renewed starting point.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "After sorting the kinds of causes, Timaeus marks the remaining discourse as a construction task: the causes now lie like material for builders, the remaining account must be woven from them, and the discourse should return briefly to the beginning before proceeding back to the present point."
textual_basis: At 69a, the passage compares the sorted kinds of causes to material lying ready for builders, says the remaining account must be woven from them, and directs the speakers to go back again briefly to the beginning and move quickly to the same point from which they arrived here.
limits: This records the structural restart and building-weaving metaphor. It does not summarize the new account that begins after the cited marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Timaeus marks a structural closure and recap: he says the account should now place a fitting ending and head on the myth, then restates that god introduced proportion and commensurability into things that had been disorderly."
textual_basis: At 69b, the speaker explicitly names the act of supplying an ending and head for the myth and immediately refers back to what was said at the beginning, where god imposed measured relations on things that previously lacked stable names such as fire and water except by chance.
limits: This records a discourse marker and recapitulatory transition. It does not claim that the whole cosmology is closed at 69b, since the account proceeds into the construction and placement of mortal soul-parts.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ordered, craftsman, mortal things, offspring, imitating, immortal, mortal
observation: "The passage restates the delegated construction sequence: god himself is the maker of divine things, commands his own offspring to make the mortal kinds, and those offspring imitate him by receiving an immortal soul-principle and building a mortal body and mortal soul-kind around it."
textual_basis: At 69c, Timaeus first says god ordered the materials and constituted the whole living thing, then distinguishes divine production from mortal production by assigning the latter to god's offspring. The subordinate makers imitate, receive the immortal beginning of soul, turn a mortal body as its vehicle, and add another mortal form of soul within the body.
limits: This records the repeated delegation and craft sequence. It does not decide whether this passage revises or merely resumes the earlier account of the younger gods.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "The mortal soul-kind is introduced through a catalogue of necessary and dangerous affections: pleasure, pains, confidence, fear, spiritedness, hope, irrational perception, and desire are mixed together as the mortal race is composed."
textual_basis: At 69d, the text lists the mortal soul's internal contents in an ordered series, beginning with pleasure and pains and ending by saying that these were mixed with irrational perception and desire in composing the mortal kind.
limits: This records the local catalogue of mortal-soul affections. It does not infer a complete psychology from this list or identify every listed term with a later anatomical location.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: to defile, divine, apart, dwelling, isthmus, boundary, neck, chest
observation: "The body is anatomically partitioned to keep the mortal soul-kind separate from the divine: the makers place the mortal kind in another bodily dwelling, build the neck as an isthmus and boundary between head and chest, and bind the mortal kind in the chest."
textual_basis: "At 69d-69e, the stated motive is avoiding defilement of the divine except where necessity requires it. The placement follows that motive: a separate bodily residence is assigned, the neck is built between head and chest as a divider, and the mortal soul-kind is set in the chest."
limits: This records the placement logic in the passage. It does not claim that spatial separation by itself explains the ethical rank of the soul-parts.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "The chest itself is further divided because the mortal soul has a better and a worse part: a diaphragm is placed between them, separating their dwellings within the thorax."
textual_basis: At 69e-70a, after saying the mortal soul-kind was bound in the chest, Timaeus says that because one part of it was naturally better and another worse, the makers again build the cavity of the thorax apart, dividing it by placing the diaphragm in the middle.
limits: This records the internal thoracic partition. It does not yet identify the lower appetitive part, since the cited span has not reached that account.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - ἐπιθυμιῶν
english_gloss: courage, spiritedness, victory-loving, head, reason, obedient, citadel, appetites
observation: The spirited part is placed nearer the head so that it can hear reason and help restrain the appetitive kind when the latter refuses voluntary obedience to commands from the citadel.
textual_basis: At 70a, the part sharing in courage and spiritedness is described as victory-loving and is settled between the diaphragm and neck, closer to the head. The purpose clause states that, being obedient to reason, it can join reason in forcefully holding back the appetites when they resist the command and speech from the citadel.
limits: This records the stated anatomical and functional relation between spiritedness, reason, and appetite. It does not import the Republic's argument for tripartition beyond the terms used in this passage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "The heart is assigned a command-and-signal role for spirited response: as source of blood and center of the veins, it is placed in a guard-station so that reason's reports can quickly carry commands and threats through perceptive channels and produce obedience."
textual_basis: Across 70a-70b, the account places the spirited soul nearer the head and between diaphragm and neck, then identifies the heart as the knot of the veins and blood-source. Its purpose clause links reason's report, spirited heat, bodily channels of perception, commands and threats, and obedience to the best part.
limits: This records the heart's signaling role in the spirited system. It does not include the lung's cooling mechanism or make the heart the seat of all cognition.
review_status: accepted
```
